else

10:

\texttt{res}[\texttt{i}]\leftarrow\texttt{DIFF}[\texttt{a}[\texttt{i}]+B][\texttt{c}]

11:

\texttt{carry}\leftarrow 1

12:

return

res

Proof of correctness.

The sum is done component by component. The result is not
necessarily a normalized integer (even if the arguments
\texttt{a},\texttt{b}
are normalized)
but we normalize it afterward: finally, the integer represented by
res
is the sum of the integers

\mathtt{val}(\texttt{a})
and
\mathtt{val}(\texttt{b})
represented by
a
and
b
if it is less than
B^{d}
(more generally, it is
\mathtt{val}(\texttt{a})+\mathtt{val}(\texttt{b})\mod B^{d}
).

For the difference, the idea is to
maintain a carry along the algorithm. When the carry is set to one,
it means that we need to subtract it from the next “digit”. Notice
that storing a carry of
1
is enough as
\texttt{b}[i]<B
and

\texttt{carry}\leq 1
and therefore
\texttt{b}[i]+\texttt{carry}\leq B\leq B+\texttt{a}[\texttt{i}]
.
Notice also that
\texttt{a}[\texttt{i}]<B
and thus
\texttt{a}[\texttt{i}]+B<2B
, which justifies that the array

DIFF
only needs to be initialized up to
2B-1
.
Finally, note that the
Difference
procedure uses not only the pre-computed array
DIFF
but also the array
LowerEQ
pre-computed in Algorithm 7 above.
∎

Multiplication:

The recipe for getting multiplication is, unsurprisingly, the same as
before: we pre-compute an array
\texttt{MULT}[0..B-1][0..B-1]
and use it with the naive multiplication algorithm.

Algorithm 9

Computation of multiplication

1:

procedure

LinPreprocMult
(
)

2:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{B}-1

do

3:

\texttt{MULT}[\texttt{x}][0]\leftarrow 0

4:

for

\texttt{y}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{B}-2

do

5:

\texttt{MULT}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}+1]\leftarrow\texttt{MULT}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]+\texttt{x}

1:

procedure

Multiply
(
\texttt{a},\texttt{b}
)

2:

\texttt{res}\leftarrow
array of size
d

3:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{d}-1

do

4:

\texttt{res}[\texttt{i}]\leftarrow 0

5:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{d}-1

do

6:

for

\texttt{j}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{d}-1-\texttt{i}

do

7:

\texttt{res}[\texttt{i}+\texttt{j}]\leftarrow\texttt{res}[\texttt{i}+\texttt{j}]+

8:

\texttt{MULT}[\texttt{a}[\texttt{i}]][\texttt{b}[\texttt{j}]]

9:

return

Normalize
(
res
)

Proof of correctness.

Here
MULT
stores a result that might be larger than
B
, which
means that in the multiplication
res
will not be normalized
but it will get normalized later. (Each cell of
res
might contain a value less than or equal to

d\times(B-1)^{2}<dN
. If that is considered too much we could also
normalize after each loop but we don’t do it here for the sake of
simplicity.)
Finally, the integer represented by
res
is the product of the integers

\mathtt{val}(\texttt{a})
and
\mathtt{val}(\texttt{b})
represented by
a
and
b
if it is less than
B^{d}
and, more generally, it is
(\mathtt{val}(\texttt{a})\times\mathtt{val}(\texttt{b}))\;\mathtt{mod}\;B^{d}
.
∎

How does the complexity of the sum, the difference, and the product depend on the size of the operands?

Clearly, all the algorithms presented above for addition, subtraction
and product run in constant time. This is due to the fact that each
instruction involves a fixed number of registers and that it is
executed a number of times bounded by the constant
d
. Of course,
more precisely, the execution time of our algorithms depends on the
number of digits (= number of registers) in the base
B=\lceil N^{1/2}\rceil
of their operands, which is at most twice their number
of digits in the base
N
. It is a simple matter to observe that the
above addition and subtraction algorithms are executed in time
O(d)

and that the multiplication algorithm of two numbers of
k
and
m

digits, respectively, runs in time
O(k(k+m))
, which is
O(d^{2})
, for

k+m=d
. Note that if
d
was large, one could fall back to any fast
multiplication algorithm such as the Fast Fourier Transform.

Remark 9
.

Note that our
Sum
(resp.
Diff
) algorithm on “polynomial’ integers
a,b<N^{d}
(or
a,b<B^{d}
, for
B=\lceil N^{1/2}\rceil
) working in time
O(d)
, can be seen as an addition (resp. subtraction) algorithm on integers
a,b<N^{\ell}
working in time
O(\ell)
, for any non-constant integer
\ell
.

In particular, suppose that
a,b
are arbitrary integers written in binary with at most
n
bits, i.e.
a,b<2^{n}
;
then, splitting their binary representations into subwords of length
\left\lceil(\log_{2}n)/2\right\rceil
(the last subword can be shorter),
a,b
can be represented in base
\beta\coloneqq 2^{\left\lceil(\log_{2}n)/2\right\rceil}
by the arrays
\texttt{a}[0..N-1]
and
\texttt{b}[0..N-1]
of
N
digits
\texttt{a}[i],\texttt{b}[i]
, where

N\coloneqq\left\lceil n/\left\lceil(\log_{2}n)/2\right\rceil\right\rceil
, with
\texttt{a}[i],\texttt{b}[i]<\beta<N
(for
n
large enough).
Obviously, our
Sum
(resp.
Diff
) algorithm applied to the arrays
\texttt{a}[0..N-1]
and
\texttt{b}[0..N-1]
, with
N
instead of
d
, computes their sum array
\texttt{s}[0..N]
(resp. difference array
\texttt{s}[0..N-1]
) in time
O(N)=O(n/\log n)
under
unit cost criterion
. Note that this is
O(n)
time (linear time) under
logarithmic cost criterion
.

5
Executing division in constant time

In this section, we present one of the most significant and surprising
result of this paper. Although division is obviously the most
difficult of the four standard operations, we will show in this
section that it can also be computed in constant time – again after
linear-time preprocessing.

The importance of this result is underlined by its consequences:

•

the following sections will show that a number of other
arithmetic operations on “polynomial” integers – square root,
general
c
th root, exponential, logarithm – can also be computed
in constant time, by using the four operations, including,
essentially, division;

•

like the other arithmetic operations, the division can be used
by some “dynamic” problems: as an example, the
paper
[
9
]
uses a particular case of division to
compute in constant time (with linear-time preprocessing) the
j
th
solution of a first-order query on a bounded-degree structure.

The novelty of our division algorithm is a very subtle case separation
which uses a form of approximation and recursive procedures whose
number of calls is bounded by a constant.

It will be convenient to represent the operands of a division sometimes in the base
K{}\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/6}\right\rceil
,
sometimes in the base
B\coloneqq K{}^{3}
. (Note that the conversion of an integer from base
K{}
to base
B
, and vice versa, is immediate.) The bounds
N^{1/2}\leq B=O(N^{1/2})
will be useful.

We say that an integer
x
is “small” if
x<B
, i.e., if it has
only one digit when represented in the base
B
.
First, let us check that this division by a “small” integer can be done in
constant time by a variant of the standard division algorithm.

5.1
Dividing a “polynomial” integer by a “small” integer

Here, we want to divide a “polynomial” integer
a\coloneqq a_{d-1}B^{d-1}+\cdots+a_{1}B+a_{0}
, represented in the base

B
, by an integer
b<B
. For that we will use Horner’s
method. Let us define the sequence
v_{d},\ldots,v_{1},v_{0}
defined
inductively by
v_{d}\coloneqq 0
and
v_{i}\coloneqq B\times v_{i+1}+a_{i}
,
for
0\leq i<d
. Note that
v_{0}=a
.

For the
v_{i}
, we can define
q_{i}
and
r_{i}
, the quotient and the
remainder of dividing
v_{i}
by
b
, for
0\leq i\leq d
. Note that

v_{0}=a
, therefore
q_{0}
is the number we are looking for. Since

v_{i}=B\times v_{i+1}+\penalty\ a_{i}
and
v_{i+1}=q_{i+1}\times b+r_{i+1}
we deduce

v_{i}=B\times q_{i+1}\times b+B\times r_{i+1}+a_{i}

Let us introduce the respective quotients
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}
and remainders
r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}<b
of the division of
a_{i}
and

B\times r_{i+1}
by
b
: we have

a_{i}=q^{\prime}\times b+r^{\prime}
and
B\times r_{i+1}=q^{\prime\prime}\times b+r^{\prime\prime}
and therefore

v_{i}=(q_{i+1}\times B+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime})\times b+r^{\prime}+r^{\prime\prime}

and thus, depending on whether
r^{\prime}+r^{\prime\prime}<b
, we have

•

q_{i}=q_{i+1}\times B+q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}
and
r_{i}=r^{\prime}+r^{\prime\prime}
in case
r^{\prime}+r^{\prime\prime}<b
,
which gives the equalities (1,2)

1.

q_{i}=q_{i+1}\times B+(a_{i}\;\mathtt{div}\;b)+(r_{i+1}\times B)\;\mathtt{div}\;b

2.

r_{i}=(a_{i}\;\mathtt{mod}\;b)+(r_{i+1}\times B)\;\mathtt{mod}\;b
,

and, in case
b\leq r^{\prime}+r^{\prime\prime}<2b
,

•

q_{i}=q_{i+1}\times B+q^{\prime\prime}+q^{\prime}+1
and
r_{i}=r^{\prime}+r^{\prime\prime}-b
.

The following algorithm uses four arrays
\texttt{D}[0..B][1..B]
,
\texttt{R}[0..B][1..B]
,

\texttt{DM}[0..B][1..B]
, and
\texttt{RM}[0..B][1..B]
, defined by

\texttt{D}[a][b]\coloneqq\lfloor a/b\rfloor
,
\texttt{R}[a][b]\coloneqq a\;\mathtt{mod}\;b
,

\texttt{DM}[a][b]\coloneqq\lfloor(a\times B)/b\rfloor
,
and
\texttt{RM}[a][b]\coloneqq(a\times B)\;\mathtt{mod}\;b
.

Algorithm 10

Computation of division
\left\lfloor\texttt{a}/\texttt{b}\right\rfloor
by small integer
(
\texttt{a}<\texttt{B{}}^{d}
and
0<\texttt{b}<\texttt{B{}}
)

1:

procedure

LinPreprocDivBySmall
(
)

2:

for

\texttt{b}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{B{}}

do

3:

\texttt{D}[0][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow 0

4:

\texttt{R}[0][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow 0

5:

for

\texttt{a}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{B}

do

6:

\texttt{D}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow\texttt{D}[\texttt{a}-1][\texttt{b}]

7:

\texttt{R}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow\texttt{R}[\texttt{a}-1][\texttt{b}]+1

8:

if

\texttt{R}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]=\texttt{b}

then

9:

\texttt{D}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow\texttt{D}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]+1

10:

\texttt{R}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow 0

11:

\texttt{DM}[0][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow 0

12:

\texttt{RM}[0][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow 0

13:

for

\texttt{a}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{B}

do

14:

\texttt{DM}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow\texttt{DM}[\texttt{a}-1][\texttt{b}]+\texttt{D}[\texttt{B}][\texttt{b}]

15:

\texttt{RM}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow\texttt{RM}[\texttt{a}-1][\texttt{b}]+\texttt{R}[\texttt{B}][\texttt{b}]

16:

if

\texttt{RM}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]\geq\texttt{b}

then

17:

\texttt{DM}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow\texttt{DM}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]+1

18:

\texttt{RM}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]\leftarrow\texttt{RM}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}]-\texttt{b}

1:

procedure

DivBySmall
(
\texttt{a},\texttt{b}
)

2:

\texttt{q}\leftarrow 0

3:

\texttt{r}\leftarrow 0

4:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ \texttt{d-1}\penalty\ \textbf{downto}\penalty\ 0

do

5:

\texttt{q}\leftarrow\texttt{q}\times\texttt{B}+\texttt{D}[\texttt{a}[\texttt{i}]][\texttt{b}]+\texttt{DM}[\texttt{r}][\texttt{b}]

6:

\texttt{r}\leftarrow\texttt{R}[\texttt{a}[\texttt{i}]][\texttt{b}]+\texttt{RM}[\texttt{r}][\texttt{b}]

7:

if

\texttt{r}\geq\texttt{b}

then

8:

\texttt{q}\leftarrow\texttt{q}+1

9:

\texttt{r}\leftarrow\texttt{r}-\texttt{b}

10:

return

q

Justification:
Clearly, the
LinPreproc
procedure correctly computes the arrays

\texttt{D},\texttt{R}
,
DM
and
RM
in time
O(B^{2})=O(N)
.
The
DivBySmall
procedure initializes the pair
(q,r)
to
(0,0)=(q_{d},r_{d})
.
Then, for each
i=d-1,\ldots,1,0
of the for loop, it transforms
(q,r)=(q_{i+1},r_{i+1})
into

(q,r)=(q_{i},r_{i})
: indeed, lines 5 and 6 implement exactly the equalities of the above items (1) and (2), respectively, and lines 7-9 implement the case distinction according to whether

r
(
=r^{\prime}+r^{\prime\prime})
is less than
b
or not.
Therefore, the return value of
q
on line 10 is
q_{0}
, as expected.
Obviously,
DivBySmall
runs in constant time.

5.2
Two fundamental “recursive” lemmas

Our division algorithm for the general case is based on the following two lemmas, which justify recursive processing.

Item 1 of Lemma
5
below expresses that if the divisor
b
is large enough then the quotient
\left\lfloor\left\lfloor a/K{}\right\rfloor/\left\lceil b/K{}\right\rceil\right\rfloor
is an approximation within 1 of the desired quotient
\left\lfloor a/b\right\rfloor
while item 2 justifies that this computation can be applied iteratively until the divisor is smaller than
K{}^{3}
, i.e. becomes a “small” integer.

Lemma 5

(first “recursive” lemma)
.

Assume
N>1
so that
K{}\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/6}\right\rceil
is at least 2.
Let
a,b
be integers such that
b\geq K{}^{3}
and
b>a/K{}
.
Let
q
be the quotient
\left\lfloor a^{\prime}/b^{\prime}\right\rfloor
where
a^{\prime}\coloneqq\left\lfloor a/K{}\right\rfloor
and
b^{\prime}\coloneqq\left\lceil b/K{}\right\rceil
.

1.

We have the following lower and upper bounds:

q\leq a/b<q+2
. In other words, we have either
\left\lfloor a/b\right\rfloor=q
or
\left\lfloor a/b\right\rfloor=q+1
.

2.

Moreover, we still have
b^{\prime}>a^{\prime}/K{}
.

Proof.

We have

q=\lfloor\lfloor a/K{}\rfloor/\lceil b/K{}\rceil\rfloor\leq\lfloor a/K{}\rfloor/\lceil b/K{}\rceil\leq(a/K{})/(b/K{})=a/b
, hence
q\leq a/b
.
There remains to show
a/b<q+2
.
We give a name to the difference between each rounded expression that appears in the expression of
q
and the value it approximates:

•

\epsilon_{1}\coloneqq a/K{}-\lfloor a/K{}\rfloor
;
\epsilon_{2}\coloneqq\lceil b/K{}\rceil-b/K{}
;

\epsilon_{3}\coloneqq\left\lfloor a/K{}\right\rfloor/\left\lceil b/K{}\right\rceil-\left\lfloor\left\lfloor a/K{}\right\rfloor/\left\lceil b/K{}\right\rceil\right\rfloor
.

Of course, we have
0\leq\epsilon_{i}<1
for each
\epsilon_{i}
and, by definition of
q
and the
\epsilon_{i}
’s,

q=(a/K{}-\epsilon_{1})/(b/K{}+\epsilon_{2})-\epsilon_{3}.

It comes
(q+\epsilon_{3})(b/K{}+\epsilon_{2})=a/K{}-\epsilon_{1}
, and then, by multiplying by
K{}
,
qb+\epsilon_{3}b+q\epsilon_{2}K{}+\epsilon_{3}\epsilon_{2}K{}=a-\epsilon_{1}K{}
. Hence, we deduce

a-qb=\epsilon_{3}b+q\epsilon_{2}K{}+\epsilon_{3}\epsilon_{2}K{}+\epsilon_{1}K{}.

Since each
\epsilon_{i}
is smaller than 1, we get the inequality

\displaystyle a-qb<b+(q+2)K{}

(1)

From the hypothesis
b>a/K{}
which can be written
a/b<K{}
and from the inequality

q\leq a/b
we deduce
q<K{}
, and then
q\leq K{}-1
,
hence
(q+2)K{}\leq K{}^{2}+K{}
which is less than
K{}^{3}
by the hypothesis
K{}\geq 2
.
From the hypothesis
b\geq K{}^{3}
, one deduces
(q+2)K{}<b

and then by (
1
)
a-qb<2b
, which can be rewritten
a/b<q+2
.
We have therefore proved item 1 of the lemma.

Let us now prove item 2. From the hypothesis
b>a/K{}
we deduce

b^{\prime}=\left\lceil b/K{}\right\rceil\geq b/K{}>(a/K{})/K{}\geq\left\lfloor a/K{}\right\rfloor/K{}=a^{\prime}/K{}
and finally
b^{\prime}>a^{\prime}/K{}
.
∎

It remains to deal with the case where the condition
b>a/K{}
of Lemma
5
is false. The idea is to return to the conditions of application of Lemma
5
and, more precisely, to the computation of a quotient
\lfloor a^{\prime}/b\rfloor
for an integer
a^{\prime}
such that
b>a^{\prime}/K{}
. This is expressed by item 2 of the following lemma.

Lemma 6

(second “recursive” lemma)
.

Assume
0<b\leq a/K{}
.
Let
q\coloneqq K{}\lfloor a/(K{}b)\rfloor
.

1.

Obviously, we have
q\leq a/b
and
\lfloor a/b\rfloor=q+\lfloor(a-qb)/b\rfloor
.

2.

We have
a-qb<K{}b
and therefore the computation of
\lfloor a/b\rfloor
amounts to the computation of

\lfloor a^{\prime}/b\rfloor
for
a^{\prime}=a-qb<K{}b
, that means
b>a^{\prime}/K{}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the inequality
a-qb<K{}b
of item 2. We have

\lfloor a/(K{}b)\rfloor>a/(K{}b)-1
, then
q=K{}\lfloor a/(K{}b)\rfloor>a/b-K{}
,
and finally
qb>a-K{}b
, which gives the expected inequality.
∎

5.3
Recursive computation of division in constant time

The “recursive” lemmas justify recursive procedures for computing the quotient
\left\lfloor a/b\right\rfloor
, for “polynomial” integers
a,b<K{}^{d}
, for a constant integer
d
.

Here, we assume that the operands
a,b
are represented in base
K{}
.
(Recall:
K{}\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/6}\right\rceil
and
B\coloneqq K{}^{3}
.)
For example,
a
is represented by the array

\texttt{a}[0..\texttt{d}-1]
of its digits
\texttt{a}[\texttt{i}]\coloneqq a_{i}<K{}
with

a=a_{d-1}K{}^{d-1}+\cdots+a_{1}K{}+a_{0}
, which is also
written
\overline{a_{d-1}\ldots a_{1}a_{0}}
. Note that the quotients

\left\lfloor a/K{}\right\rfloor
and
\left\lceil b/K{}\right\rceil
involved in
Lemma
5
are computed in a straightforward way: we
have
\left\lfloor a/K{}\right\rfloor=a_{d-1}K{}^{d-2}+\cdots+a_{2}K{}+a_{1}
, that means the

d
-digit representation of
a/K{}
is the “right shift”

\overline{0a_{d-1}\ldots a_{2}a_{1}}
; moreover, the following identity
can be easily verified:

\displaystyle\left\lceil b/K{}\right\rceil=\left\lfloor(b-1)/K{}\right\rfloor+1

(2)

Dividing by an integer
b>a/K{}
.

Lemma
5
completed by identity (
2
)
proves that the following recursive procedure correctly computes

\left\lfloor a/b\right\rfloor
when
a
and
b
are “close”, which means
b>a/K{}
.

Algorithm 11

Computation of division for “close” operands

1:

procedure

divClose
(
\texttt{a},\texttt{b}
)

\triangleright

a/K{}<b<K^{d}
and
a<K^{d}

2:

if

\texttt{b}<K{}^{3}

then

\triangleright
recall
B=K{}^{3}

3:

return

DivBySmall
(
\texttt{a},\texttt{b}
)

4:

else

\triangleright
here,
b\geq K{}^{3}
and
b>a/K{}
: apply Lemma
5

5:

\texttt{q}\leftarrow\textsc{divClose}(\textsc{DivBySmall}(\texttt{a},K),\textsc{DivBySmall}(b-1,K)+1)

6:

if

\texttt{a}<(\texttt{q}+1)\times\texttt{b}

then

7:

return

q

8:

else

9:

return

\texttt{q}+1

The general division algorithm.

Lemma
6
justifies that the following recursive
procedure which uses the previous procedure
divClose

correctly computes
\left\lfloor a/b\right\rfloor
for
b>0
in the general case.

Algorithm 12

Computation of division

1:

procedure

divide
(
\texttt{a},\texttt{b}
)

\triangleright

a,b<K^{d}
and
b>0

2:

if

\texttt{b}\times K>\texttt{a}

then

3:

return

DivClose
(
\texttt{a},\texttt{b}
)

4:

else

\triangleright
here,
0<b\leq a/K{}
: apply Lemma
6

5:

\texttt{q}\leftarrow K\times\textsc{divide}(\texttt{a},K\times\texttt{b})

6:

return

\texttt{q}+\textsc{DivClose}(\texttt{a}-\texttt{q}\times\texttt{b},\texttt{b})

Lemma 7
.

The time complexities of the procedures
divClose
and

divide
are respectively
O(d^{3})
and
O(d^{4})
.

Proof.

The complexity of those procedures mainly depends on the number of recursive calls they make.

Complexity of

divClose
: If

K{}^{3}\leq b<K{}^{d}
and
b>a/K{}
then the
execution of
\textsc{divClose}(a,b)
calls the same procedure

divClose
with the second argument
b
replaced by

\left\lceil b/K{}\right\rceil\leq K{}^{d-1}
, etc.
The number of nested recursive calls until the second argument of
divClose
becomes less than
K{}^{3}
is therefore at most
d-2
.

The part of the procedure
divClose
that takes the most time is
the line 6 of its code which computes the product

{(\texttt{q}+1)\times\texttt{b}}
in time
O(d^{2})
. Since

\textsc{divClose}(a,b)
executes line 6 once for each recursive call and therefore less than
d
times, its total time
complexity is
O(d^{3})
.

Complexity of

divide
: The execution
of
\textsc{divide}(a,b)
, for
a,b<K{}^{d}
and

b\leq a/K{}<K{}^{d-1}
, calls

\textsc{divide}(a,b\times K{})
, which calls

\textsc{divide}(a,b\times K{}^{2})
, etc. Due to the obvious inequality
b\times K{}^{d}>a
,
the execution of
\textsc{divide}(a,b)
stops with the stack of recursive calls to the
procedure
divide
of height at most
d
.

The most time consuming part of the procedure
divide
is the line
5
of its code which is executed as many times as the number
of the nested recursive calls of the form
divide
(
\texttt{a},K{}\times\texttt{b}
) that it makes. This number is at most
d
. It is also the number of calls of the form
divClose
(
a-q\times b,b
), line
6
, each of which is executed in time
O(d^{3})
.
Thus, the time of the procedure
divide
is
O(d^{4})
.
∎

As a consequence of the previous results including Lemma
7
, the following perhaps surprising theorem is
established.

Theorem 2
.

Let
d\geq 1
be a fixed integer.
There is a RAM with addition, such that, for any input integer
N
:

1.

Pre-computation:
the RAM computes some tables in time

O(N)
;

2.

Division:
using these pre-computed tables and reading any
integers
a,b<N^{d}
with
b>0
, the RAM computes in constant time

O(d^{4})
the quotient
\lfloor a/b\rfloor
and the remainder
a\;\mathtt{mod}\;b
.

Using the fact that the usual four operations, addition, subtraction, product and, most importantly, division, are computable in constant time on a RAM with addition, we can now also design constant time algorithms for several other important operations on integers: square or cubic root (rounded), exponential function and rounded logarithm, etc.

Convention
.

For simplicity, we will henceforth write
x/y
to denote the Euclidean quotient

\textsc{divide}(x,y)=\left\lfloor x/y\right\rfloor
in the algorithms.

6
Computing exponential and logarithm in constant time

The next examples of arithmetic operations (on “polynomial” operands)
that we will prove to be computable in constant time are the logarithm
function
(x,y)\mapsto\left\lfloor\log_{x}y\right\rfloor
and the exponential function

(x,y)\mapsto x^{y}
provided the result
x^{y}
is also “polynomial”.

6.1
Computing exponential

We want to compute
z=x^{y}
, for all integers
x,y<N^{d}
such that the result
z
is less than
N^{d}
, for a constant integer
d\geq 1
. In other words, we are going to compute the function

\mathtt{exp}_{N,d}
from
[0,N^{d}[^{2}
to
[0,N^{d}[\cup\{\mathtt{OVERFLOW}\}
defined by:

\mathtt{exp}_{N,d}:(x,y)\mapsto\begin{cases}x^{y}&\mathtt{if}\;x^{y}<N^{d}\\
\mathtt{OVERFLOW}&\mathtt{otherwise}\end{cases}

Here, we will use the two integers
B\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/2}\right\rceil
and
L_{d}\coloneqq\lceil\log_{2}(N^{d})\rceil=\lceil d\log_{2}N\rceil
computable in time
O(N)
.

Pre-computation.

We are going to compute two arrays:

•

the array
\texttt{BOUND}[2..B-1]
such that

\texttt{BOUND}[x]\coloneqq\mathtt{max}\{y\mid x^{y}<N^{d}\}
or, equivalently,

\texttt{BOUND}[x]\coloneqq\lceil\log_{x}(N^{d})\rceil-1
(note that
\texttt{BOUND}[x]<L_{d}
);

•

the two-dimensional array
\texttt{EXP}[2..B-1][1..L_{d}-1]
such that

\texttt{EXP}[x][y]\coloneqq x^{y}
for all integers
x,y
such that

2\leq x<B=\left\lceil N^{1/2}\right\rceil
and
1\leq y\leq\texttt{BOUND}[x]
.

In other words, the arrays
BOUND
and
EXP
are defined so that the following equality is true:

\{(x,y,z)\in[2,B[\times[0,N^{d}[^{2}\;\mid\;x^{y}=z\}=\{(x,y,z)\mid x\in[2,B[\;\land\;1\leq y\leq\texttt{BOUND}[x]\;\land\;\texttt{EXP}[x][y]=z\}.

The following code computes the arrays
BOUND
and
EXP
.

Algorithm 13

Pre-computation for
exponential

1:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 2\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ B-1

do

2:

\texttt{y}\leftarrow 0

3:

\texttt{z}\leftarrow x

4:

while

z<N^{d}

do

5:

y\leftarrow y+1

6:

\texttt{EXP}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]\leftarrow z

7:

\texttt{z}\leftarrow\texttt{z}\times\texttt{x}

8:

\texttt{BOUND}[\texttt{x}]\leftarrow\texttt{y}

Justification:
One easily verifies inductively that each time the algorithm arrives at line 4, we have
x^{y}\times x=z
, so that the assignment at line 6 means

\texttt{EXP}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]\leftarrow\texttt{x}^{\texttt{y}}
.
This also explains why we have
x^{y}<N^{d}\leq x^{y+1}
at line 8, which justifies the assignment
\texttt{BOUND}[\texttt{x}]\leftarrow\texttt{y}
.

Linear-time complexity of the pre-computation:

Clearly, for each
x\in[2,B[
, the body of the while loop is repeated
i
times, where
i
is the greatest integer
i\leq\log_{x}(N^{d})
, or, equivalently,
i=\lfloor d\log_{x}N\rfloor
, which is at most
\lfloor d\log_{2}N\rfloor\leq L_{d}
. It follows that the pre-computation runs in time and space
O(B\times L_{d})=O(N^{1/2}\times d\log_{2}N)=O(N)
.

Main procedure:

The following remark justifies that the
exponential
procedure given here below correctly computes the function
\texttt{exp}_{N,d}
.

Remark 10
.

If we have
x^{y}<N^{d}
,
x\geq 2
and
y\geq 2d
then we also have
2\leq x<N^{1/2}

and
2d\leq y<d\log_{2}N\leq L_{d}
.

Proof.

Note that
x^{y}<N^{d}
and
y\geq 2d
imply
x^{2d}\leq x^{y}<N^{d}
and consequently
x<N^{1/2}
.
Also, note that
x^{y}<N^{d}
and
x\geq 2
imply
2^{y}\leq x^{y}<N^{d}
and therefore
y<\log_{2}(N^{d})
.
∎

Algorithm 14

Computation of
exponential

1:

procedure

exponential
(
\texttt{x},\texttt{y}
)

2:

if

\texttt{x}=1
or
\texttt{y}=0

then

3:

return
1

4:

if

\texttt{x}=0

then

5:

return
0

6:

if

\texttt{y}<2d

then

\triangleright

x\geq 2

7:

\texttt{z}\leftarrow 1

8:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ {1}\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ {\texttt{y}}

do

9:

\texttt{z}\leftarrow\texttt{x}\times\texttt{z}

\triangleright

z=x^{i}

10:

if

\texttt{z}\geq\texttt{N}^{d}

then

11:

return

\mathtt{OVERFLOW}

\triangleright

x^{y}\geq x^{i}\geq\texttt{N}^{d}

12:

return

z

\triangleright

z=x^{y}

13:

if

\texttt{x}<\texttt{B}
and
\texttt{y}\leq\texttt{BOUND}[\texttt{x}]

then

14:

return

\texttt{EXP}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]

\triangleright
the correct value by the definition of the array
EXP

15:

else

\triangleright
either
y\geq 2d
and
x\geq B
, or
y>\texttt{BOUND}[x]
for
x\geq 2
; therefore
x^{y}\geq N^{d}

16:

return

\mathtt{OVERFLOW}

Constant complexity of the
exponential
procedure:

Clearly, the most time consuming part is the for loop, lines 8-11. It makes at most
2d
multiplications of integers
x,z<N^{d}
, each in time
O(d^{2})
. Therefore, the procedure runs in time
O(d^{3})
.

6.2
Computing logarithm

We want to compute
\left\lfloor\log_{x}y\right\rfloor
, for all integers
x\in[2,N^{d}[
and
y\in[1,N^{d}[
, where
d\geq 1
is a constant integer.

Here again, we use the base
B\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/2}\right\rceil
.

Preliminary lemmas

Lemma 8
.

For any integer
x\geq 2
and any real
a\geq 2
, we have

\log_{x}(a)-1<\log_{x}\lfloor a\rfloor\leq\log_{x}(a)
.

Proof.

We have
a/x\leq a/2\leq a-1
and therefore

\log_{x}(a)-1=\log_{x}(a/x)\leq\log_{x}(a-1)<\log_{x}\lfloor a\rfloor\leq\log_{x}(a)
.
∎

Here again, our main procedure will be recursive. It is based on the following Lemma.

Lemma 9
.

For all integers
x\geq 2
,
\ell\geq 1
and
y\geq 2x^{\ell}
, we have

\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor=\begin{cases}\lfloor\log_{x}\lfloor y/x^{\ell}\rfloor\rfloor+\ell&(1)\\
\mathtt{or}&\\
\lfloor\log_{x}\lfloor y/x^{\ell}\rfloor\rfloor+\ell+1&(2)\end{cases}

Proof.

Lemma
8
applied to
a=y/x^{\ell}\geq 2

gives
\log_{x}(y/x^{\ell})-1<\log_{x}\lfloor y/x^{\ell}\rfloor\leq\log_{x}(y/x^{\ell})
.
Adding
\ell
to each member of those inequalities yields

\log_{x}(y)-1<\log_{x}\lfloor y/x^{\ell}\rfloor+\ell\leq\log_{x}(y).

This can be rewritten:

\log_{x}\lfloor y/x^{\ell}\rfloor+\ell\leq\log_{x}(y)<\log_{x}\lfloor y/x^{\ell}\rfloor+\ell+1
.
Taking the integer parts, we obtain

\lfloor\log_{x}\lfloor y/x^{\ell}\rfloor\rfloor+\ell\leq\lfloor\log_{x}(y)\rfloor\leq\lfloor\log_{x}\lfloor y/x^{\ell}\rfloor\rfloor+\ell+1.

∎

How to determine which case (1) or (2) of Lemma
9
is true?
The following lemma completes Lemma
9
by giving a simple criterion.

Lemma 10
.

Let
s\coloneqq\lfloor\log_{x}\lfloor y/x^{\ell}\rfloor\rfloor+\ell
for integers
x\geq 2
,
\ell\geq 1
and
y\geq 2x^{\ell}
.
Lemma
9
says (1)
\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor=s
or (2)
\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor=s+1
.
If
x^{s+1}>y
then
\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor=s
. Otherwise,
\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor=s+1
.

Proof.

(1) means
x^{s}\leq y<x^{s+1}
and (2) means
x^{s+1}\leq y<x^{s+2}
.
So,
x^{s+1}>y
implies (1), and
x^{s+1}\leq y
implies (2).
∎

Principle of the algorithm and pre-computation in linear time.

For each
x\in[2,B]
, let us define

\mathtt{L}[x]\coloneqq\lceil\log_{x}B\rceil=\mathtt{min}\{z\in\mathbb{N}\mid x^{z}\geq B\}
.

x\leq B
implies
\texttt{L}[x]\geq 1
.
Clearly, the array
\texttt{L}[2..B]
can be pre-computed in time
O(B\log_{2}B)=O(N)

by the following algorithm where an invariant of the while loop is
y=x^{z}
.

Algorithm 15

Computation of the array
L

1:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 2\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ B

do

2:

\texttt{y}\leftarrow\texttt{x}

3:

\texttt{z}\leftarrow 1

4:

while

\texttt{y}<B

do

\triangleright

y=x^{z}<B

5:

\texttt{y}\leftarrow\texttt{x}\times\texttt{y}

6:

\texttt{z}\leftarrow\texttt{z}+1

\triangleright

x^{z-1}<B\leq y=x^{z}

7:

\texttt{L}[\texttt{x}]\leftarrow\texttt{z}

The algorithm that computes
\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor
, for integers
x\in[2,N^{d}[
and
y\in[1,N^{d}[
, divides into the following four cases:

1.

x>y
: then
\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor=0
;

2.

x\leq y<2B
: then
\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor
will be computed by a simple look up in a pre-computed array
\mathtt{LOGAR}[2..2B-1][2..2B-1]
defined by

\mathtt{LOGAR}[x][y]\coloneqq\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor
, for
2\leq x\leq y<2B
;

3.

x\leq y
and
2B\leq y<2x^{L[x]}
:
then we have
x\leq y<2x^{L[x]}<2x^{1+\log_{x}B}=2xB
;
this implies
1\leq\left\lfloor y/x\right\rfloor<2B
, and because of the identity
9
9

9

Indeed,
z=\left\lfloor\log_{x}y\right\rfloor
means
x^{z}\leq y<x^{z+1}
, which implies

x^{z-1}\leq\left\lfloor y/x\right\rfloor<x^{z}
and therefore
z-1\leq\left\lfloor\log_{x}\left\lfloor y/x\right\rfloor\right\rfloor<z
. That means
z=\left\lfloor\log_{x}\left\lfloor y/x\right\rfloor\right\rfloor+1
.

\left\lfloor\log_{x}y\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\log_{x}\left\lfloor y/x\right\rfloor\right\rfloor+1
, the value of
\left\lfloor\log_{x}y\right\rfloor
is obtained by computing
\left\lfloor\log_{x}\left\lfloor y/x\right\rfloor\right\rfloor
according to cases 1 or 2;

4.

y \geq 2x^{L[x]}
: then apply Lemma
10
recursively with
\ell\coloneqq L[x]
(noting that the required assumptions
x\geq 2
,
\ell\geq 1
and
y\geq 2x^{\ell}
are satisfied).

We claim that the following code computes the array
LOGAR
:

Algorithm 16

Computation of the array
LOGAR

1:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 2\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ 2\times B-1

do

2:

\texttt{y}\leftarrow\texttt{x}

3:

\texttt{z}\leftarrow 1

4:

\texttt{t}\leftarrow\texttt{x}\times\texttt{x}

5:

while

\texttt{y}<2\times B

do

6:

while

\texttt{y}<\texttt{t}

do

7:

\texttt{LOGAR}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]\leftarrow\texttt{z}

8:

\texttt{y}\leftarrow\texttt{y}+1

9:

\texttt{z}\leftarrow\texttt{z}+1

10:

\texttt{t}\leftarrow\texttt{x}\times\texttt{t}

Indeed, it can be checked inductively that each time the execution of the algorithm reaches line 7 then we have
x^{z} \leq y<x^{z+1}=t
, which justifies the assignment

\texttt{LOGAR}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]\leftarrow\texttt{z}
.
It is also easy to verify that the algorithm runs in time
O(B^{2})=O(N)
.

The main algorithm.

We assert that the following procedure
logarithm

correctly computes
\lfloor\log_{x}y\rfloor
, for all the integers
x\in[2,N^{d}[
and
y\in[1,N^{d}[
.

Algorithm 17

Computation of
logarithm

1:

procedure

logarithm
(
x
,
y
)

2:

if

\texttt{y}<\texttt{x}

then

\triangleright
case 1

3:

return
0

4:

if

\texttt{y}<2\times B

then

\triangleright
case 2

5:

return

\texttt{LOGAR}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]

6:

if

\texttt{y}<2\times\textsc{exponential}(\texttt{x},\texttt{L}[x])

then

\triangleright
case 3:
1\leq y/x<2B

7:

return

\textsc{logarithm}(\texttt{x},\texttt{y}/\texttt{x})+1

8:

else

\triangleright
case 4 (
y\geq 2x^{L[x]}
): apply Lemma
10
with
\ell=L[x]

9:

\texttt{s}\leftarrow\textsc{logarithm}(\texttt{x},\texttt{y}/\textsc{exponential}(\texttt{x},\texttt{L}[\texttt{x}]))+\texttt{L}[\texttt{x}]

10:

if

\textsc{exponential}(\texttt{x},\texttt{s}+1)>\texttt{y}

then

11:

return

s

12:

else

13:

return

\texttt{s}+1

Justification:
Obviously, the
logarithm
procedure is correct for
y<x
(case 1: lines 2-3) and for
x\leq y<2B
(case 2: lines 4-5).
Lines 6-7 treats the case 3,
x\leq y<2x^{L[x]}
(recall
L[x]=\left\lceil\log_{x}B\right\rceil
), which implies
1\leq y/x<2B
: we therefore return to cases 1 or 2 by taking
y/x
instead of
y
. This justifies line 7.
The case
y\geq 2x^{L[x]}
(case 4: lines 8-13) is justified by Lemma
10
by taking
\ell\coloneqq L[x]
and noting that the required assumptions
x\geq 2
,
\ell\geq 1
and
y\geq 2x^{\ell}
are satisfied.

Constant time and space complexity of the
logarithm
procedure.

Let us first note that all the numbers (operands, intermediate values, results) manipulated by the procedure are less than
N^{2d}
and that all the arithmetic operations it uses can be computed in constant time and space. In particular, this holds for the “exponential” expressions
2x^{\mathtt{L}[x]}<2xB\leq N^{d+1}
and
x^{s+1}\leq xy<N^{2d}
,
which involve the function
\mathtt{exp}_{N,2d}
of the previous subsection, computed by the
exponential
procedure in constant time and space. Therefore, it suffices to prove that the
logarithm
procedure performs at most a constant number of recursive calls.
We have
x^{\texttt{L}[x]}\geq B
, by definition of
\texttt{L}[x]
.
Therefore, for
y<N^{d}\leq B^{2d}
, the algorithm cannot divide
y
by

x^{\texttt{L}[x]}\geq B
more than
2d-1
times. This implies that it makes at most
2d-1
recursive calls (by case 4) before reaching a value of
y
less than
x
(basic case 1), or less than
2B
(basic case 2), or less than
2x^{L[x]}
(case 3, which performs one recursive call leading to case 1 or case 2).
Thus, for a fixed
d
, the
logarithm
procedure runs in constant time and space.

Open problem: computing the modular exponentiation.

Obviously, the functions
(x,y,z)\mapsto x+y\mod z
, and

(x,y,z)\mapsto x\times y\mod z
, for
x,y<N^{d}
and
1\leq z\leq N^{d}
, are computable in constant time. Thus, a natural candidate for
being computable (or not) in constant time is the following version of
the exponential function, widely used in cryptography: the function

ExpMod
from
[0,N^{d}[^{2}\times[1,N^{d}]
to
[0,N^{d}[
defined,
for
x,y<N^{d}
and
1\leq z\leq N^{d}
, by

\textsc{ExpMod}(x,y,z)\coloneqq x^{y}\mod z.

Now, we cannot prove that
\textsc{ExpMod}(x,y,z)
is computable in
constant time, even when fixing
x=c
or
z=N^{d}
, for constant integers
c>1
,
d\geq 1
.
Indeed, it seems likely that even the following functions are
not computable in constant time after a linear preprocessing, for
x,y,z<N
:

(y,z)\mapsto 2^{y}\mod z\hskip 30.00005pt(x,y)\mapsto x^{y}\mod N^{2}\hskip 30.00005pt\mathrm{or}\;\mathrm{even}\;\;\;(x,y)\mapsto x^{y}\mod N.

Intuitively, the irregularity of the modulo function applied to a
large number - an exponential - prevents us from designing an
induction by approximation as we have done for division and logarithm
and as we will for the square root and the other roots in the next
section.

7
Computing the square root and other roots in constant time

In this section, we will show that, for a fixed integer
c\geq 2
, the

c
-th root of a “polynomial” integer can be computed in constant
time.
For “small” integers (i.e. less than
N
), it is easy to compute
during a linear time preprocessing a table giving the square roots or
the
c
-th roots
\lfloor x^{1/c}\rfloor
, for any fixed integer

c\geq 2
but here we will show that we can generalize to polynomial
integers using a discrete adaptation of Newton’s approximation method.

7.1
The
c
-th root algorithm

Let
c\geq 2
be a fixed integer. Let us explain our strategy to compute the

c
-th root of an integer
x<N^{d}
, for a constant integer
d
, i.e.
the function
x\mapsto\lfloor x^{1/c}\rfloor
on the domain

[0,N^{d}[
.

Similarly to the division method, our algorithm will distinguish
“small values” for which the
c
-th root will be pre-computed and
“large values” for which we will compute an approximation (using
recursively the
cthRoot
function with smaller values) before
improving this approximation.

The preprocessing here is similar to that of the division: we have a bound

\texttt{M}^{\prime}
and we populate an array
cthRoot
such that

\texttt{cthRoot}[\texttt{x}]=\left\lfloor\texttt{x}^{1/c}\right\rfloor
for all

0\leq\texttt{x}<\texttt{M}^{\prime}
. To compute the
c
-th root for an
x
below
this
\texttt{M}^{\prime}
limit, we return its pre-computed value. For an
x
over
this
\texttt{M}^{\prime}
limit, we first compute
t
which is the floored

c
-th root of the Euclidean quotient
\left\lfloor\texttt{x}/\texttt{K}^{c}\right\rfloor
.
Note that the flooring happens twice: we floor the quotient

\texttt{x}/\texttt{K}^{c}
and we floor the
c
-th root of
\texttt{x}/\texttt{K}^{c}
.
Overall, we only know that the value
\texttt{x}^{1/c}
is in the range

[\texttt{t}\times\texttt{K},(\texttt{t}+1)\times\texttt{K}[
.
As we will show below, the following two points are essential:

•

by a careful choice of
K
and
\texttt{M}^{\prime}
, this is a sufficiently good approximation and it suffices to update this approximation by using two steps of Newton’s method and one step of checking whether the answer is
g
or
\texttt{g}-1
where

g
is our current approximation;

•

the values for
K
and
\texttt{M}^{\prime}
can also be chosen such that

\texttt{M}^{\prime}=O(N)
, which allows a linear preprocessing, and
\texttt{K}=\Theta(N^{1/(2c)})
, which ensures that the procedure executes in constant time.

We know present the algorithm whose
correctness is justified in the next paragraphs.

Algorithm 18

Computation of
x\mapsto\left\lfloor x^{1/c}\right\rfloor
using the pre-computed constants
\texttt{M}^{\prime}

and
K

1:

procedure

LinPreprocCthRoot
(
)

2:

\texttt{cthRoot}\leftarrow
an array of size
\texttt{M}^{\prime}

3:

\texttt{cthRoot}[0]\leftarrow 0

4:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{M}^{\prime}-1

do

5:

\texttt{s}\leftarrow\texttt{cthRoot}[\texttt{x}-1]

6:

if

\texttt{x}<(\texttt{s}+1)^{c}

then

7:

\texttt{cthRoot}[\texttt{x}]\leftarrow\texttt{s}

8:

else

9:

\texttt{cthRoot}[\texttt{x}]\leftarrow\texttt{s}+1

1:

procedure

improve
(
\texttt{g},\texttt{x}
)

2:

if

\texttt{g}^{c}\leq\texttt{x}<(\texttt{g}+1)^{c}

then

3:

return

g

4:

else

5:

return

(\texttt{x}+(c-1)\times\texttt{g}^{c})/(c\times\texttt{g}^{c-1})

1:

procedure

cthRoot
(
x
)

2:

if

\texttt{x}<\texttt{M}^{\prime}

then

3:

return

\texttt{cthROOT}[\texttt{x}]

4:

else

5:

\texttt{s}\leftarrow\texttt{x}/\texttt{K}^{c}

6:

\texttt{t}\leftarrow\textsc{cthRoot}(\texttt{s})

7:

\texttt{g}_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{t}\times\texttt{K}

8:

\texttt{g}_{1}\leftarrow\textsc{improve}(\texttt{g}_{0},\texttt{x})

9:

\texttt{g}_{2}\leftarrow\textsc{improve}(\texttt{g}_{1},\texttt{x})

10:

if

\texttt{g}_{2}^{c}\leq\texttt{x}<(\texttt{g}_{2}+1)^{c}

then

11:

return

\texttt{g}_{2}

12:

else

13:

return

\texttt{g}_{2}-1

Setting the integers
K
and
\texttt{M}^{\prime}
:

Here, we will simply use
\texttt{K}\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/(2c)}\right\rceil
, which is the integer
K
such that

(\texttt{K}-1)^{2c}<N\leq\texttt{K}^{2c}
, and let
\texttt{M}\coloneqq\texttt{K}^{2c}
.
(Note that
K
and
M
can be easily computed in time
O(N)
.)
The value of
\texttt{M}^{\prime}
will be set as

\texttt{M}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathtt{max}(\texttt{M},c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c})
, for
c_{0}
a constant specified in the next paragraph, which only depends on
c
.
Note that, for any given
c
, we have

\texttt{M}=\texttt{M}^{\prime}
for any
N
large enough, which
justifies the linearity of the preprocessing, and we have

\texttt{K}^{c}\geq N^{1/2}
, which, considering lines 5-6 of the
cthRoot
procedure, will justify that the recursion depth is at most

2d
for an integer
x<N^{d}
(and thus the
cthRoot
procedure is constant
time for any fixed
d
).

The mysterious constant
c_{0}
:

We define the constant integers

c_{0}\coloneqq\left\lceil\left\lceil\sqrt{6c-12}\;\right\rceil\times(c^{2}-1)/(6c)\right\rceil
and
c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c}
. This allows us to
define
M^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathtt{max}(M,c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c})
. The role of the
strange constants
c_{0}
and
c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c}
will be elucidated at
the end of the proof of the algorithm. Note that for
c=2
, we have

c_{0}=0
and therefore
M^{\prime}=M
, and for
c=3
, we have
c_{0}=2
and

c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c}=72
. More generally, for any fixed
c
, the values

c_{0}
and
c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c}
are explicit integers.

7.2
Correctness and complexity of the
c
-th root algorithm

First, note the inequality, for all
a\geq 0
and
c\geq 1
:

(a+1)^{c}+1\leq\left(a+\dfrac{c+1}{c}\right)^{c}

(3)

It follows from the (in)equalities

\left(a+\frac{c+1}{c}\right)^{c}=\left((a+1)+\frac{1}{c}\right)^{c} \geq(a+1)^{c}+c(a+1)^{c-1}\times\frac{1}{c}=(a+1)^{c}+(a+1)^{c-1}\geq(a+1)^{c}+1
.

When
x<M^{\prime}
, the
cthRoot
procedure returns
\texttt{cthROOT}[x]
, which is the correct value. Otherwise, we have
x\geq K^{2c}
and
x\geq c_{0}^{2c}
.
The integers
s
and
t
will be such that
t^{c}\leq s<(t+1)^{c}
.
Because of
t^{c}\leq s\leq x/K^{c}<s+1
and
g_{0}=tK
and inequality (
3
) for
a=t
,
we obtain:
g_{0}^{c}=t^{c}K^{c}\leq x<(s+1)K^{c}<(\left(t+1\right)^{c}+1)K^{c}\leq\left(t+\dfrac{c+1}{c}\right)^{c}K^{c}=\left(tK+\dfrac{c+1}{c}K\right)^{c}

and then
g_{0}^{c}\leq x<\left(g_{0}+\dfrac{c+1}{c}K\right)^{c}
.
Hence,
g_{0}\leq x^{1/c}<g_{0}+\dfrac{c+1}{c}K
.
Let
\epsilon_{0}
denote the real number such that

x^{1/c}=g_{0}\times(1+\epsilon_{0}).

(4)

We have
0\leq\epsilon_{0}<\dfrac{K(c+1)}{cg_{0}}=\dfrac{c+1}{ct}
.

Starting from the approximate value
g_{0}
of
x^{1/c}
, let us apply Newton’s method: let
g_{1}
be the abscissa of the point of ordinate 0 of the tangent to the point
(g_{0},f(g_{0}))
of the function
X\mapsto f(X)=X^{c}-x
. This means that
g_{1}
is the number satisfying the equation:

f^{\prime}(g_{0})=\dfrac{0-f(g_{0})}{g_{1}-g_{0}}

(5)

This gives
cg_{0}^{c-1}=\dfrac{x-g_{0}^{c}}{g_{1}-g_{0}}
, and (multiplying by
g_{1}-g_{0}
),

cg_{1}g_{0}^{c-1}-cg_{0}^{c}=x-g_{0}^{c}
, and therefore
cg_{1}g_{0}^{c-1}=(c-1)g_{0}^{c}+x
, and finally

g_{1}=\dfrac{1}{c}\left((c-1)g_{0}+\dfrac{x}{g_{0}^{c-1}}\right)

(6)

From (
6
) and (
4
) we get

g_{1}=\dfrac{(c-1)g_{0}+(1+\epsilon_{0})^{c}\times g_{0}}{c}=g_{0}(1+\epsilon_{0})\times\dfrac{c+c\epsilon_{0}+(1+\epsilon_{0})^{c}-1-c\epsilon_{0}}{c+c\epsilon_{0}}\leq\linebreak x^{1/c}\times\left(1+\dfrac{\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{0}^{i}}{c}\right)=x^{1/c}\times\left(1+\epsilon_{0}^{2}\times\dfrac{\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{0}^{i-2}}{c}\right)
. We deduce

x^{1/c}\leq g_{1}\leq x^{1/c}\times\left(1+\epsilon_{0}^{2}/c\times\left(\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{0}^{i-2}\right)\right).

(7)

Let us compare two consecutive summands
S_{i},S_{i+1}
of the sum

\sum_{i=2}^{c}S_{i}
where
S_{i}\coloneqq\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{0}^{i-2}
. For
2\leq i<c
, we have

S_{i+1}=S_{i}\times\dfrac{(c-i)\epsilon_{0}}{i+1}\leq S_{i}\times\dfrac{(c-2)\epsilon_{0}}{3}
,
from which we deduce
S_{i+1}/S_{i}\leq\dfrac{(c-2)\epsilon_{0}}{3}\leq\dfrac{(c-2)(c+1)}{3ct}

because of
\epsilon_{0}\leq\dfrac{c+1}{ct}
. The quotient
S_{i+1}/S_{i}

is at most 1 if we assume

t\geq c_{1}
for the constant
c_{1}\coloneqq\dfrac{(c-2)(c+1)}{3c}
.

Under this assumption, each of the
c-1
summands
S_{i}=\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{0}^{i-2}
,
2\leq i\leq c
, is less than or equal to
S_{2}=\binom{c}{2}=\frac{c(c-1)}{2}
, so that we obtain

\dfrac{\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{0}^{i-2}}{c}\leq\dfrac{1}{c}\times(c-1)\times\dfrac{c(c-1)}{2}=\dfrac{(c-1)^{2}}{2}
and, as another consequence of
\epsilon_{0}\leq\dfrac{c+1}{ct}
,

\epsilon_{0}^{2}\times\dfrac{\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{0}^{i-2}}{c}\leq\dfrac{(c^{2}-1)^{2}}{2c^{2}t^{2}}.

(8)

Note that in the
cthRoot
procedure, the variable
\texttt{g}_{1}
represents the integer part
\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor
of
g_{1}
.
We know that
x^{1/c}\leq g_{1}
but it is possible that

\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor\leq x^{1/c}
. In this case, we have
\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor\leq x^{1/c}\leq g_{1}
,
which implies
\texttt{g}_{1}=\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor=\lfloor x^{1/c}\rfloor
, that means
\texttt{g}_{1}
is the value we are looking for.
Otherwise, we have
\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor>x^{1/c}
and we denote
\epsilon_{1}
the positive number such that

\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor=x^{1/c}\times(1+\epsilon_{1})
.
From the inequalities
x^{1/c}\leq x^{1/c}(1+\epsilon_{1})\leq g_{1}\leq x^{1/c}\times\left(1+\epsilon_{0}^{2}/c\times\left(\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{0}^{i-2}\right)\right)
and (
8
), we deduce

0\leq\epsilon_{1}\leq\dfrac{(c^{2}-1)^{2}}{2c^{2}t^{2}}

(9)

When
\texttt{g}_{1}=\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor>\lfloor x^{1/c}\rfloor

we iterate Newton’s method by using the approximate value
\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor

and, by replacing
g_{0}
by
\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor
in expression (
6
), we obtain the integer part
\texttt{g}_{2}=\lfloor g_{2}\rfloor
of the number

g_{2}\coloneqq\dfrac{1}{c}\times\left((c-1)\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor+\dfrac{x}{\lfloor g_{1}\rfloor^{c-1}}\right).

Then, we have

g_{2}=\dfrac{1}{c}\times\left((c-1)x^{1/c}(1+\epsilon_{1})+\dfrac{x}{x^{(c-1)/c}\times(1+\epsilon_{1})^{c-1}}\right)=\dfrac{x^{1/c}}{c}\times\;\dfrac{(c-1)(1+\epsilon_{1})^{c}+1}{(1+\epsilon_{1})^{c-1}}=x^{1/c}\times\;\dfrac{(c-1)(1+c\epsilon_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{1}^{i})+1}{c(1+\epsilon_{1})^{c-1}}=x^{1/c}\times\;\dfrac{c+c(c-1)\epsilon_{1}+(c-1)(\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{1}^{i})}{c+c(c-1)\epsilon_{1}+c(\sum_{i=2}^{c-1}\binom{c-1}{i}\epsilon_{1}^{i})}\leq\linebreak x^{1/c}\times\left(1+\dfrac{c-1}{c}\times\left(\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{1}^{i-2}\right)\times\epsilon_{1}^{2}\right)
.
Finally, we obtain

x^{1/c}\leq g_{2}\leq x^{1/c}\times\left(1+\dfrac{c-1}{c}\times\left(\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{1}^{i-2}\right)\times\epsilon_{1}^{2}\right)

(10)

With the same argument as above, the quotient between two consecutive summands of the sum

\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{1}^{i-2}
is not greater than
\dfrac{(c-2)\epsilon_{1}}{3}
, which is at most
\dfrac{(c-2)(c^{2}-1)^{2}}{6c^{2}t^{2}}
because of (
9
).
This quotient is not greater than 1 if we assume
t^{2}\geq\dfrac{(c-2)(c^{2}-1)^{2}}{6c^{2}}
,
that means

t\geq\dfrac{\sqrt{6c-12}\times(c^{2}-1)}{6c}
or, if we assume, more strongly,
t\geq c_{0}
, by definition of
c_{0}
.

Therefore, if we assume
t\geq c_{0}
, then each of the
c-1
summands
\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{1}^{i-2}
,
2\leq i\leq c
, is less than or equal to the first summand
\binom{c}{2}=\dfrac{c(c-1)}{2}
so that one deduces the inequality

\dfrac{c-1}{c}\times\left(\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{1}^{i-2}\right)\leq\dfrac{c-1}{c}\times(c-1)\times\dfrac{c(c-1)}{2}=\dfrac{(c-1)^{3}}{2}

and, as another consequence of (
9
),

\dfrac{c-1}{c}\times\left(\sum_{i=2}^{c}\binom{c}{i}\epsilon_{1}^{i-2}\right)\times\epsilon_{1}^{2}\leq\dfrac{(c-1)^{3}}{2}\times\dfrac{(c^{2}-1)^{4}}{4c^{4}t^{4}}=\dfrac{(c-1)^{7}(c+1)^{4}}{8c^{4}t^{4}}.

(11)

From the inequalities (
10
) and (
11
), we deduce

x^{1/c}\leq g_{2}\leq x^{1/c}\times\left(1+\dfrac{c_{2}}{t^{4}}\right)

where

c_{2} \coloneqq\dfrac{(c-1)^{7}(c+1)^{4}}{8c^{4}}
.

Remark 11
.

Recall that those inequalities hold
under the hypotheses

t\geq c_{1}=(c-2)(c+1)/(3c)
and
t\geq c_{0}
. Also, note that the hypothesis
t\geq c_{0}
is sufficient since we have
c_{0}>c_{1}
for
c\geq 2
, which is easy but tedious to prove.

Proof of
x^{1/c}\leq g_{2}<x^{1/c}+1
(under the hypothesis
t\geq c_{0}
).

We have
x^{1/c}\leq g_{2}\leq x^{1/c}+\epsilon_{3}

for
\epsilon_{3}\coloneqq\dfrac{c_{2}x^{1/c}}{t^{4}}
.
Besides, we have

t=\left\lfloor\left\lfloor\dfrac{x}{K^{c}}\right\rfloor^{1/c}\right\rfloor>\left(\dfrac{x}{K^{c}}\right)^{1/c}-1=\dfrac{x^{1/c}-K}{K}

(because we have
\left\lfloor\left\lfloor a\right\rfloor^{1/c}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor a^{1/c}\right\rfloor>a^{1/c}-1
, for each positive real number
a
).
We deduce
\epsilon_{3}=\dfrac{c_{2}x^{1/c}}{t^{4}}\leq\dfrac{c_{2}K^{4}x^{1/c}}{(x^{1/c}-K)^{4}}=c_{2}\times\dfrac{K^{4}((x^{1/c}-K)+K)}{(x^{1/c}-K)^{4}}=c_{2}\times\left(\dfrac{K^{4}}{(x^{1/c}-K)^{3}}+\dfrac{K^{5}}{(x^{1/c}-K)^{4}}\right)
.
Recall the hypothesis
x\geq K^{2c}
for
K=\left\lceil N^{1/(2c)}\right\rceil
.
This implies
x^{1/c}-K\geq K^{2}-K
and
\epsilon_{3}\leq c_{2}\times\left(\dfrac{K^{4}}{(K^{2}-K)^{3}}+\dfrac{K^{5}}{(K^{2}-K)^{4}}\right)=c_{2}\times\left(\dfrac{K}{(K-1)^{3}}+\dfrac{K}{(K-1)^{4}}\right)=c_{2}\times\dfrac{K^{2}}{(K-1)^{4}}
. We obtain
\epsilon_{3}\leq c_{2}\times\dfrac{K^{2}}{(K-1)^{4}}
.

For
K\geq 1+2\sqrt{c_{2}}

10
10

10

That means

M=K^{2c}\geq\left(1+(c-1)^{7/2}\times(c+1)^{2}/(\sqrt{2}\times c^{2})\right)^{2c}
.
For instance, it means
M\geq 46
for
c=2
and
M\geq 12\penalty\ 405\penalty\ 543
for
c=3
.
,
we get

\dfrac{K^{2}}{(K-1)^{4}}=\dfrac{1}{(K-1)^{2}}+\dfrac{2}{(K-1)^{3}}+\dfrac{1}{(K-1)^{4}}\leq\dfrac{1}{4c_{2}}+\dfrac{2}{4c_{2}}+\dfrac{1}{4c_{2}}=\dfrac{1}{c_{2}}
, which implies

\epsilon_{3}\leq c_{2}\times\dfrac{K^{2}}{(K-1)^{4}}\leq 1
.
Therefore, we obtain
x^{1/c}\leq g_{2}\leq x^{1/c}+1
, or, equivalently,

g_{2}-1\leq x^{1/c}\leq g_{2}
.

This implies that we have
either
\left\lfloor x^{1/c}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor g_{2}\right\rfloor=\texttt{g}_{2}
or

\left\lfloor x^{1/c}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor g_{2}\right\rfloor-1=\texttt{g}_{2}-1
.
This justifies the last line of the
cthRoot
procedure and completes the proof of its correctness
if
we can prove that the hypothesis
t\geq c_{0}

always
holds when
x\geq M^{\prime}
.

Why
x\geq M^{\prime}
implies
t\geq c_{0}
.

Let us prove this implication by contradiction. Assume we have both
x\geq M^{\prime}
and
t<c_{0}
.
We have
x\geq M=K^{2c}
and
x\geq c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c}
.

Recall that
s=\left\lfloor x/K^{c}\right\rfloor
and
t=\left\lfloor s^{1/c}\right\rfloor
imply
x/K^{c}<s+1
and
s^{1/c}<t+1
, which means
s<(t+1)^{c}
.
With the hypothesis
x\geq M=K^{2c}
, this yields

K^{c}\leq x/K^{c}<s+1<(t+1)^{c}+1
and therefore
K^{c}\leq(t+1)^{c}
.
Finally, using the hypothesis
t<c_{0}
, we get
K\leq t+1<c_{0}+1
and
K\leq c_{0}
.

Besides, from the hypothesis
x\geq c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c}
and from
K\leq c_{0}
, we deduce, for
s=\lfloor x/K^{c}\rfloor
, the inequalities:

s>x/K^{c}-1\geq(c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c})/K^{c}-1\geq(c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c})/c_{0}^{c}-1=c_{0}^{c}
, hence
s>c_{0}^{c}
and
t=\lfloor s^{1/c}\rfloor\geq\lfloor(c_{0}^{c})^{1/c}\rfloor=c_{0}
.
Therefore, we get
t\geq c_{0}
, a contradiction.

The proof of the correctness of the
cthRoot
procedure is now complete.
\square

Proof of the constant time of the
cthRoot
procedure.

For
x<N^{d}\leq K^{2cd}
, the algorithm (see lines 5-6) can divide
x
by
K^{c}
at most
2d-2
times before reaching a value
x<M=K^{2c}
for which the
c
-th root is given by

\texttt{cthRoot}[x]
. This means that the
cthRoot
procedure makes at most
2d-2
recursive calls and therefore, for a fixed
d
, executes in constant time.
\square

Finally, we have proved that any root of any “polynomial” integer can be computed in constant time. More precisely:

Proposition 3
.

Let
c\geq 2
and
d\geq 1
be fixed integers.
There is a RAM with addition such that, for any input integer
N
:

1.

Pre-computation:
the RAM computes some tables in time
O(N)
;

2.

Computation of the root:
using these pre-computed tables and reading any integer
x<N^{d}
, the RAM computes in constant time the
c
-th root
\lfloor x^{1/c}\rfloor
.

7.3
Generalized root: a conjecture partially resolved positively

Although we are unable to prove it, we believe that Proposition
3

can be generalized to
y
th root, for an integer variable
y<N^{d}
.

Conjecture 1
.

For any fixed integer
d\geq 1
, the function
(x,y)\mapsto\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor
, where
x<N^{d}
and
2\leq y<N^{d}
, is computable in constant time after preprocessing in
O(N)
time.

In this subsection, we give strong arguments in favor of this conjecture.

Remark 12
.

The conjecture is true for the restriction of the function
(x,y)\mapsto\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor
to the following subdomains:

1.

y\geq\log_{2}N
: then
0\leq\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor<2^{d}
(because for
z=\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor
we have

z^{\log_{2}N}\leq z^{y}\leq x<N^{d}=2^{d\log_{2}N}
, which implies
z<2^{d}
) and therefore,

\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor
can be computed in time
O(d)
by dichotomic search;

2.

y\leq(\log_{2}N)/(12\log_{2}\log_{2}N)
: then the algorithm of subsection
7.1
computing the
c
th root
\left\lfloor x^{1/c}\right\rfloor
, for
x<N^{d}
and any fixed integer
c
, and its justification (subsection
7.2
) can be slightly adapted.

Since the conjecture is also answered positively for
y<\log_{2}N
and
x<N^{1-\varepsilon}
, for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
, by pre-computing in time
O(N)
an array
root
of dimensions

N^{1-\varepsilon}\times\log_{2}N
, in fact the only open case is

\log_{2}N/(12\log_{2}\log_{2}N)<y<\log_{2}N
with
N^{1-\varepsilon}\leq x<N^{d}
.

This yields the following algorithm, which makes free use of the exponential and (rounded) logarithm functions proven to be computed in constant time in Section
6
.

Algorithm 19

Computation of
(x,y)\mapsto\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor
, for
x<\texttt{N}^{d}
and
(
2\leq y<\texttt{L}/(12\left\lceil\log_{2}\texttt{L}\right\rceil)
or
\texttt{L}<y<\texttt{N}^{d}
),
where
\texttt{L}\coloneqq\lfloor\log_{2}\texttt{N}\rfloor

1:

procedure

LinPreprocRoot
(
)

2:

\texttt{B}\leftarrow\mathtt{max}(\left\lceil\texttt{N}^{1/4}\right\rceil^{3},\left\lceil\texttt{N}^{1/(d+1)}\right\rceil^{d}+1)

3:

\texttt{L}\leftarrow\lfloor\log_{2}\texttt{N}\rfloor

4:

\texttt{RootN}\leftarrow
an array of dimension
L

5:

\texttt{root}\leftarrow
an array of dimensions
\texttt{B}\times\texttt{L}

6:

for

\texttt{y}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{L}

do

7:

\texttt{r}\leftarrow 0

8:

while

\texttt{r}^{3\times\texttt{y}}<\texttt{N}

do

9:

\texttt{r}\leftarrow\texttt{r}+1

10:

\texttt{RootN}[\texttt{y}]\leftarrow\texttt{r}

\triangleright

r=\lceil N^{1/(3\times y)}\rceil

11:

for

\texttt{y}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{L}

do

12:

\texttt{root}[0][\texttt{y}]\leftarrow 0

13:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{B}-1

do

14:

\texttt{s}\leftarrow\texttt{root}[\texttt{x}-1][\texttt{y}]

15:

if

\texttt{x}<(\texttt{s}+1)^{\texttt{y}}

then

16:

\texttt{root}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]\leftarrow\texttt{s}

17:

else

18:

\texttt{root}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]\leftarrow\texttt{s}+1

1:

procedure

improve
(
\texttt{g},\texttt{x},\texttt{y}
)

2:

if

\texttt{g}^{\texttt{y}}\leq\texttt{x}<(\texttt{g}+1)^{\texttt{y}}

then

3:

return

g

4:

else

5:

return

(\texttt{x}+(\texttt{y}-1)\times\texttt{g}^{\texttt{y}})/(\texttt{y}\times\texttt{g}^{\texttt{y}-1})

1:

procedure

root
(
\texttt{x},\texttt{y}
)

2:

if

\texttt{y}>\texttt{L}

then

\triangleright

0\leq\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor<2^{d}

3:

\texttt{r}\leftarrow 0

4:

\texttt{u}\leftarrow 2^{d}

5:

while

\texttt{u}-\texttt{r}>1

do

6:

\texttt{z}\leftarrow(\texttt{r}+\texttt{u})/2

7:

if

\texttt{x}<\texttt{z}^{\texttt{y}}

then

8:

\texttt{u}\leftarrow\texttt{z}

9:

else

10:

\texttt{r}\leftarrow\texttt{z}

return

r

12:

if

\texttt{y}<\texttt{L}/(12\times\left\lceil\log_{2}\texttt{L}\right\rceil)

then

13:

if

\texttt{x}<\texttt{B}

then

return

\texttt{root}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]

16:

\texttt{s}\leftarrow\texttt{x}/(\texttt{RootN}[\texttt{y}])^{\texttt{y}}

17:

\texttt{t}\leftarrow\textsc{root}(\texttt{s},\texttt{y})

18:

\texttt{g}_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{t}\times\texttt{RootN}[\texttt{y}]

19:

\texttt{g}_{1}\leftarrow\textsc{improve}(\texttt{g}_{0},\texttt{x},\texttt{y})

20:

\texttt{g}_{2}\leftarrow\textsc{improve}(\texttt{g}_{1},\texttt{x},\texttt{y})

21:

if

\texttt{g}_{2}^{\texttt{y}}\leq\texttt{x}<(\texttt{g}_{2}+1)^{\texttt{y}}

then

\texttt{g}_{2}

else

\texttt{g}_{2}-1

Justification of the algorithm.

The case
y>\texttt{L}
, for
\texttt{L}=\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor
, lines 2-11 of the
root
procedure, is justified by the following invariant condition:
r^{y}\leq x<u^{y}

(meaning
0\leq x<2^{dy}
at the initialization):
therefore, the return value
r
, obtained after at most
d
iterations of the body of the while loop, satisfies
r^{y}\leq x<(r+1)^{y}
as required.

The case
y<\texttt{L}/(12\times\left\lceil\log_{2}\texttt{L}\right\rceil)
, lines 12-24 of the
root
procedure,
is a variant of the
cthRoot
procedure, where the fixed integer
c
is replaced by the integer variable
y
. Moreover, the computations which justify the correctness of this case are the same as those of
cthRoot
, see Subsection
7.2
, with the following adaptations.

•

Instead of setting
K\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/(2c)}\right\rceil
, we set
K\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/(3y)}\right\rceil
, which we pre-compute and store as an array element:
\texttt{RootN}[y]\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/(3y)}\right\rceil
.

•

The recursive case of of the
root
procedure is
x\geq B
, for

B\coloneqq\mathtt{max}(\left\lceil N^{1/4}\right\rceil^{3},\left\lceil N^{1/(d+1)}\right\rceil^{d}+1)
, instead of
x\geq M^{\prime}
, for

M^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathtt{max}(M,c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{2c})

(with
M\coloneqq K^{2c}
and

c_{0}\coloneqq\left\lceil\left\lceil\sqrt{6c-12}\;\right\rceil\times(c^{2}-1)/(6c)\right\rceil
).

•

Our computations continue to use the numbers
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2}
introduced in Subsection
7.2
, now defined by replacing
c
by
y
. In particular, we now set

c_{0}\coloneqq\left\lceil\left\lceil\sqrt{6y-12}\;\right\rceil\times(y^{2}-1)/(6y)\right\rceil
and

c_{2} \coloneqq(y-1)^{7}\times(y+1)^{4}/(8y^{4})
.

For the reasoning and computations of Subsection
7.2
to still be valid, it suffices to check that the following three conditions hold under the assumption

y<L/(12\times\left\lceil\log_{2}L\right\rceil)
, for sufficiently large integers
y
and
N
:

1.

B\geq K^{2y}
;

2.

B\geq c_{0}^{2y}+c_{0}^{y}
;

3.

K\geq 1+2\sqrt{c_{2}}
.

It is important to note that, by items 1 and 2, the condition
x\geq B
implies
x\geq\mathtt{max}(K^{2y},c_{0}^{2y}+c_{0}^{y})
.
This corresponds to the condition

x\geq M^{\prime}=\mathtt{max}(K^{2c},c_{0}^{2c}+c_{0}^{c})
of the
cthRoot
procedure.
Recall also that, to conclude the reasoning of Subsection
7.2
, condition 3 (
K\geq 1+2\sqrt{c_{2}}
) has been assumed.

Proof of item 1.

Since we have
K=\left\lceil N^{1/(3y)}\right\rceil\geq 2
for
N\geq 2
, we obtain

K/2\leq K-1<N^{1/(3y)}
. This implies
K<2N^{1/(3y)}
and then

K^{2y}<2^{2y}\times N^{2/3}
.
From the assumption
y<L/(12\times\left\lceil\log_{2}L\right\rceil)
, we get

2^{2y}<2^{L/(6\left\lceil\log_{2}L\right\rceil)}\leq 2^{(\log_{2}N)/(6\left\lceil\log_{2}L\right\rceil)}\leq N^{1/(6\left\lceil\log_{2}L\right\rceil)}
and thus
2^{2y}<N^{1/(6\left\lceil\log_{2}L\right\rceil)}
.
Moreover, for
N\geq 16
, we have
L=\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor\geq 4
and then
\left\lceil\log_{2}L\right\rceil\geq 2
. This gives
2^{2y}<N^{1/12}
.
All in all, we get
K^{2y}<2^{2y}\times N^{2/3}<N^{1/12}\times N^{2/3}=N^{3/4}\leq B
, and

B\geq K^{2y}
as claimed.
∎

The following lemma will be useful to prove items 2 and 3.

Lemma 11
.

For all numbers
y,z
and
\alpha
such that
y\geq 2
,
z>1
and
0<\alpha\leq 1
, we have the implication

y<\alpha\times z/\log_{2}z\Rightarrow y\log_{2}y<\alpha\times z

Proof of the lemma.

Assume
y\leq\alpha\times z/\log_{2}z
.
Using also the inequalities
\log_{2}\log_{2}y\geq 0
and
\log_{2}\alpha\leq 0
, we obtain

\dfrac{y\log_{2}y}{\log_{2}(y\log_{2}y)}=\dfrac{y\log_{2}y}{\log_{2}y+\log_{2}\log_{2}y}\leq y\leq\dfrac{\alpha\times z}{\log_{2}z}\leq\dfrac{\alpha\times z}{\log_{2}z+\log_{2}\alpha}=\dfrac{\alpha\times z}{\log_{2}(\alpha\times z)}
, and thus

\dfrac{y\log_{2}y}{\log_{2}(y\log_{2}y)}\leq\dfrac{\alpha\times z}{\log_{2}(\alpha\times z)}
.
Noticing that the function
x\mapsto\dfrac{x}{\log_{2}x}
is strictly increasing on its domain, we obtain
y\log_{2}y\leq\alpha\times z
as required.
∎

As a direct application of Lemma
11
where we take
z=L=\left\lfloor log_{2}N\right\rfloor>1
and
\alpha=1/12
, we obtain

Lemma 12
.

If
y<L/(12\times\left\lceil\log_{2}L\right\rceil)
then
y\log_{2}y<L/12
.

Proof of item 2 (
B\geq c_{0}^{2y}+c_{0}^{y}
).

We have

c_{0}=\left\lceil\left\lceil\sqrt{6y-12}\;\right\rceil\times(y^{2}-1)/(6y)\right\rceil<\dfrac{(\sqrt{6y-12}+1)\times(y^{2}-1)}{6y}+1<\dfrac{(\sqrt{y-2}+1/\sqrt{6})\times y+\sqrt{6}}{\sqrt{6}}
.
If
y\geq 6
, which implies
\sqrt{6}\leq y/\sqrt{6}
and
2/\sqrt{6}<2\leq\sqrt{y-2}
, we obtain
c_{0}<\dfrac{(\sqrt{y-2}+2/\sqrt{6})\times y}{\sqrt{6}}<\dfrac{2\sqrt{y-2}\times y}{\sqrt{6}}<y^{3/2}
.
This implies
c_{0}^{2y}+c_{0}^{y}<2\times c_{0}^{2y}<\linebreak 2\times(y^{3/2})^{2y}=2\times y^{3y}=2\times 2^{3y\log_{2}y}<2^{4y\log_{2}y}

(note that
y\geq 2
implies
y\log_{2}y>1
) and therefore

c_{0}^{2y}+c_{0}^{y}<2^{4y\log_{2}y}
.
Because of
y\log_{2}y<L/12
according to Lemma
12
,
we get
\log_{2}y<(1/12)L/y
and then
4y\log_{2}y<(1/3)L
.
All in all, we deduce
c_{0}^{2y}+c_{0}^{y}<2^{(1/3)L}\leq N^{1/3}<B
and then

B\geq c_{0}^{2y}+c_{0}^{y}
.
∎

Proof of item 3 (
K\geq 1+2\sqrt{c_{2}}
).

We have

1+2\sqrt{c_{2}}=1+\dfrac{(y-1)^{7/2}\times(y+1)^{2}}{\sqrt{2}\times y^{2}}
.
Moreover, it is easy to verify that for
y\geq 6
, we have
\dfrac{(y+1)^{2}}{\sqrt{2}\times y^{2}}<1
.
It comes
1+2\sqrt{c_{2}}<1+(y-1)^{7/2}<y^{7/2}
, which implies

1+2\sqrt{c_{2}}<2^{(7/2)\log_{2}y}
. Because of
y\log_{2}y<L/12
according to Lemma
12
, we get
\log_{2}y<(1/12)L/y
and then

1+2\sqrt{c_{2}}<2^{(7/2)\times(1/12)\times L/y}<2^{L/(3y)}\leq N^{1/(3y)}\leq\left\lceil N^{1/(3y)}\right\rceil=K
.
We have proved
K\geq 1+2\sqrt{c_{2}}
.
∎

Justification of the complexity.

In the
LinPreprocRoot
procedure, for each
y\in[1,L]
, the while loop, lines 8-9, does at most
N^{1/(3y)}
iterations.
Because of
N^{1/(3y)}\leq N^{1/3}
, the running time of the for loop, lines 6-10, is

O(L\times N^{1/3})=O(N)
.
Since the run time of lines 11-18 (of
LinPreprocRoot
) is
O(L\times B)=O(N)
, all preprocessing runs in time
O(N)
.

To analyze the time of the
root
procedure, it suffices to determine its recursive depth.
We have
(\texttt{RootN}(y))^{y}=\left\lceil N^{1/(3y)}\right\rceil^{y} \geq N^{1/3}
.
Since we cannot divide the integer
x<N^{d}
by
(\texttt{RootN}(y))^{y}

(which is an integer greater than
N^{1/3}
)
more than
3\times d
times, the running time of the
root
procedure is
O(d)
, which is constant for fixed
d
.

Conveniently, the notations used must be recalled and completed:

Notation
.

Let
L
,
\lambda
and
B
denote the integers
L\coloneqq\left\lfloor\log_{2}N\right\rfloor
,

\lambda\coloneqq L/(12\times\left\lceil\log_{2}L\right\rceil)
, and, for a fixed integer
d\geq 1
,

B\coloneqq\mathtt{max}(\left\lceil N^{1/4}\right\rceil^{3},\left\lceil N^{1/(d+1)}\right\rceil^{d}+1)
.

In addition to the above algorithm, the root
\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor
can also be computed in constant time for “most” integers
y
in the “remaining” interval
[\lambda,L]
.
This is the consequence of the following two lemmas.

Lemma 13
.

For all positive integers
x,p,q
, the identity

\left\lfloor x^{1/(p\times q)}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\left\lfloor x^{1/p}\right\rfloor^{1/q}\right\rfloor
holds.

Proof.

Let
u,v
denote the successive roots
u\coloneqq\left\lfloor x^{1/p}\right\rfloor
and
v\coloneqq\left\lfloor u^{1/q}\right\rfloor
.
That means
u^{p}\leq x\leq(u+1)^{p}-1
and
v^{q}\leq u\leq(v+1)^{q}-1
, from which we deduce

v^{p\times q}\leq u^{p}\leq x\leq(u+1)^{p}-1\leq((v+1)^{q}-1+1)^{p}-1=(v+1)^{p\times q}-1
. That gives

v^{p\times q}\leq x\leq(v+1)^{p\times q}-1
, which means
v=\left\lfloor x^{1/(p\times q)}\right\rfloor
as expected.
∎

Lemma 14
.

Let
d\geq 1
be a fixed integer. Suppose
N
is large enough for the inequality

\lambda^{2}/d^{2}>L
to hold. Let
y
be an integer in
[\lambda,L]
, which is not of the form, called
forbidden form
11
11

11

In particular, for
d=1
(resp.
d=2
), an integer is of the forbidden form iff it is prime (resp. it is prime or twice a prime number).
In general, by the repartition theorem of prime numbers, there are asymptotically about
d\times L/(\ln L)
integers of forbidden form among the integers in
[\lambda,L]
, which is only

O((\log N)/(\log\log N))
.
,
p\times q
for a prime number
p
and an integer
q\leq d
. Then,
y
has a divisor
y_{1}
such that
d<y_{1}<\lambda
.

Proof.

Let us consider the decomposition of
y
into prime factors,
y=\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}
, with
k\geq 2
and
p_{1}\geq p_{2}\geq\cdots\geq p_{k}
. Let
i_{0}
(resp.
i_{1}
) be the least index such that

\prod_{i=1}^{i_{0}}p_{i}>d
(resp.
\prod_{i=1}^{i_{1}}p_{i}\geq\lambda
). For
N
large enough, we have
\lambda\geq d
, which implies
i_{0}\leq i_{1}
. We have two cases:

1.

i_{0}<i_{1}
: taking
y_{1}=\prod_{i=1}^{i_{0}}p_{i}
, we obtain (by definition of
i_{0}
and
i_{1}
)
d<y_{1}<\lambda
;

2.

i_{0}=i_{1}
: then, we get
\prod_{i=1}^{i_{0}}p_{i}=\prod_{i=1}^{i_{1}}p_{i}\geq\lambda
and
\prod_{i=1}^{i_{0}-1}p_{i}\leq d
(by definition of
i_{0}
and
i_{1}
), which gives, by division,

p_{i_{0}}\geq\lambda/d
. It implies
i_{0}=1
, since otherwise, we would have

L\geq y\geq p_{i_{0}-1}\times p_{i_{0}}\geq p_{i_{0}}^{2}\geq\lambda^{2}/d^{2}
, which contradicts the inequality
\lambda^{2}/d^{2}>L
, for
N
large enough.
Thus, we obtain the factorization
y=p_{1}\times y_{1}
, where
p_{1}\geq\lambda
and

y_{1}\coloneqq\prod_{i=2}^{k}p_{i}
.
We get
y_{1}<\lambda
, since otherwise, we would have

y=p_{1}\times y_{1}\geq\lambda^{2}>L
, a contradiction. Moreover, the inequality
y_{1}>d
also holds, since otherwise,
y=p_{1}\times y_{1}
would be of the “forbidden form”.

Thus, in both cases, we can exhibit a divisor
y_{1}
of
y
such that
d<y_{1}<\lambda
.
∎

Notice that we can compute in time
O(L\times\lambda)=O(N)
an array

\texttt{factor}[\lambda..L]
defined by
\texttt{factor}[y]\coloneqq y_{1}
, where
y_{1}
is the least divisor of
y
in
[d+1,\lambda-1]
, if it exists (by Lemma
14
), and is 0 otherwise, i.e. when
y
is of the forbidden form: it suffices to check, for all
y_{1}\in[d+1,\lambda-1]
, if

y\;\mathtt{mod}\;y_{1}=0
.

Now, using the
factor
array and Lemma
13
, it is easy to compute

\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor
in constant-time (with linear-time preprocessing), for every
x<N^{d}
and every

y\in[\lambda,L]
not in forbidden form:
take
y_{1}=\texttt{factor}[y]
and
y_{2}=y/y_{1}
, which gives

\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\left\lfloor x^{1/y_{1}}\right\rfloor^{1/y_{2}}\right\rfloor
;
the roots
z\coloneqq\left\lfloor x^{1/y_{1}}\right\rfloor
and then

\left\lfloor z^{1/y_{2}}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\left\lfloor x^{1/y_{1}}\right\rfloor^{1/y_{2}}\right\rfloor

can be computed respectively by the above algorithm, lines 12-24, and by reading the element

\texttt{root}[z,y]
of the array
\texttt{root}[0..B-1][1..L]
, since we have respectively
y_{1}<\lambda
and

y_{1}\geq d+1
, from which
z=\left\lfloor x^{1/y_{1}}\right\rfloor\leq(N^{d})^{1/(d+1)}\leq\left\lceil N^{1/(d+1)}\right\rceil^{d}<B
is deduced.

Remark 13
.

Using Lemmas
14
and
13
, it is easy to verify that Conjecture
1
is equivalent to its following restriction:
For any fixed integer
d\geq 1
, the function
(x,y)\mapsto\left\lfloor x^{1/p}\right\rfloor
, where
x<N^{d}
and
p
is a prime number such that
\log_{2}N/(12\log_{2}\log_{2}N)<p<\log_{2}N
, is computable in constant time after preprocessing in
O(N)
time. Indeed, an integer in forbidden form being of the form
y=p\times q
, for a prime number
p
and an integer
q\leq d
, the computation of

\left\lfloor x^{1/y}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\left\lfloor x^{1/p}\right\rfloor^{1/q}\right\rfloor
is reduced to the computation of
z\coloneqq\left\lfloor x^{1/p}\right\rfloor
since, obviously, the final result
\left\lfloor z^{1/q}\right\rfloor
can be computed in constant time.

8
Computing many other operations in constant time

Real computers treat the contents and addresses of registers as binary
words. In addition to arithmetic operations and for the sake of
efficiency, assembly languages as well as programming languages
(Python, etc.) use string operations and bitwise logical operations:

\mathtt{and}
,
\mathtt{or}
,
\mathtt{xor}
, etc.

The objects processed by the RAM model are integers but can be assumed
to be in binary notation, i.e. they can be seen as binary words. In
this section, we show that all the usual string and logical operations
on binary words can be computed in constant time with linear-time
preprocessing, just like on a standard CPU of a real computer.

8.1
String operations and bitwise logical operations computed in constant time

We identify each integer
x\in\mathbb{N}
with the string

x_{\ell-1}\cdots x_{1}x_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, of its binary notation with

x=\sum_{0\leq i<\ell}2^{i}\times x_{i}
.
The length
\ell
of the representation of
x
(without useless leading zeros:
x_{\ell-1}>0
) is given by the
following function:

\textsc{length}(x)=\begin{cases}1&\mathtt{if}\;x=0\\
\lfloor\log_{2}x\rfloor+1&\mathtt{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Remark 14
.

We have
\textsc{length}(x)\leq\log_{2}N+O(1)
, for each integer
x=O(N)

contained in a RAM register, and
\textsc{length}(x)=O(\log N)
for each
“polynomial” integer
x=O(N^{d})
, for a fixed
d
.

Three operations are essential for manipulating binary strings:

•

the concatenation of two strings
X
and
Y
is defined as

\textsc{Conc}(X,Y)\coloneqq X\times 2^{\textsc{length}(Y)}+Y
;

•

the function that maps each pair
(X,i)
of a string
X
and an index
i
to the
i
th bit
x_{i}
of
X
is defined as

\textsc{Bit}(X,i)\coloneqq(X\;\mathtt{div}\;2^{i})\;\mathtt{mod}\;2
;

•

the substring function which associates to each string

X=x_{\ell-1}\cdots x_{1}x_{0}
and two indices
i,j
with
\ell\geq i>j\geq 0

the (nonempty) substring
x_{i-1}x_{i-2}\cdots x_{j}
, is defined as

\textsc{Substring}(X,i,j)\coloneqq(X\;\mathtt{mod}\;2^{i})\;\mathtt{div}\;2^{j}
.

With
Substring
, one can express that a string
X
is a suffix
or a prefix of another string
Y
:

•

X
is a suffix of
Y\iff X=\textsc{Substring}(Y,\textsc{length}(X),0)
;

•

X
is a prefix of
Y\iff\begin{cases}\textsc{length}(X)\leq\textsc{length}(Y)\text{ and }\\
X=\textsc{Substring}(Y,\textsc{length}(Y),\textsc{length}(Y)-\textsc{length}(X))\end{cases}
.

Remark 15
.

Note that these procedures can be straightforwardly adapted if the
integers are represented in a fixed base
\gamma\geq 2
, i.e. are
identified with strings
x_{\ell-1}\ldots x_{1}x_{0}\in\{0,1,\ldots,\gamma-1\}^{\ell}
.

Processing is similar for each bitwise logical operation. As an
example, let us study the operation
\mathtt{xor}
applied to two integers

X,Y
which are less than
N^{d}
(for a fixed
d
) and can therefore be identified with the
strings of their binary notations
x_{\ell-1}\cdots x_{0}
and

y_{\ell-1}\cdots y_{0}
of length
\ell=\textsc{length}(\mathtt{max}(X,Y))=O(\log N)
.

The natural idea is to decompose each operand
X,Y<2^{\ell}
into its

\ell
bits copied in
\ell
registers, to compute the bits
z_{i}=x_{i}\;\mathtt{xor}\;y_{i}
in
\ell
registers too, and to get the result
Z=X\;\mathtt{xor}\;Y
by concatenating the
\ell
bits
z_{i}
in one register:

Z=z_{\ell-1}\cdots z_{0}
. Obviously, this process takes time

O(\ell)
, which is, generally, not a constant time.

To compute the integer
X\;\mathtt{xor}\;Y
in constant time, for operands
X,Y<N^{d}
where
d
is a fixed integer, our trick is to
notice that if we set
X=X_{1}\times p+X_{0}
and
Y=Y_{1}\times p+Y_{0}
, where

p
is a power of two such that
X_{0},Y_{0}<p
, then we get

\displaystyle X\;\mathtt{xor}\;Y=(X_{1}\;\mathtt{xor}\;Y_{1})\times p+(X_{0}\;\mathtt{xor}\;Y_{0})

(12)

Of course, the same “decomposition” property holds for the other bitwise operations
\mathtt{and}
,

\mathtt{or}
:
X\;\mathtt{and}\;Y=(X_{1}\;\mathtt{and}\;Y_{1})\times p+(X_{0}\;\mathtt{and}\;Y_{0})

and
X\;\mathtt{or}\;Y=(X_{1}\;\mathtt{or}\;Y_{1})\times p+(X_{0}\;\mathtt{or}\;Y_{0})
.
Also, note that those equalities hold for all pairs of integers
X,Y>1
, i.e. represented with at least 2 bits.

Now, by using equation (
12
) iteratively for
p=2
, we can pre-compute a
2-dimensional table, called
xorAr
, giving
Z=X\;\mathtt{xor}\;Y
for all the pairs of small enough operands
X,Y
.
For large operands, we use equation (
12
)
for
p=2^{\ell}
and
\ell=\left\lfloor\frac{1}{2}\times\textsc{length}(\mathtt{max}(X,Y))\right\rfloor
.
Thus, we obtain a recursive procedure called
xor
:
at each step the maximum length
L
of the arguments of
xor
,

L\coloneqq\textsc{length}(\mathtt{max}(X,Y))
, is divided by two,
i.e.
L
becomes
\left\lfloor L/2\right\rfloor
or
\left\lceil L/2\right\rceil
.

The previous arguments justify that the following algorithm correctly computes the
\mathtt{xor}
operation.

Algorithm 20

Computation of the
\mathtt{xor}
operation

1:

procedure

LinPreprocXOR
(
)

2:

\texttt{K}\leftarrow{\textsc{sqrt}(N)}

\triangleright

\texttt{K}=\left\lceil\sqrt{N}\right\rceil

3:

\texttt{xorAr{}}\leftarrow
array of dimension
\texttt{K}\times\texttt{K}

4:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ 1

do

5:

for

\texttt{j}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ 1

do

6:

\texttt{xorAr{}}[\texttt{i}][\texttt{j}]\leftarrow(\texttt{i}+\texttt{j})\;\mathtt{mod}\;2

7:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{K}-1

do

8:

for

\texttt{j}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{K}-1

do

9:

if

\texttt{i}>1
or
\texttt{j}>1

then

10:

\texttt{xorAr{}}[\texttt{i}][\texttt{j}]\leftarrow 2\times\texttt{xorAr{}}[\texttt{i}/2][\texttt{j}/2]

+\;\texttt{xorAr{}}[\texttt{i}\;\mathtt{mod}\;2][\texttt{j}\;\mathtt{mod}\;2]

1:

procedure

xor
(
\texttt{x},\texttt{y}
)

2:

if

\texttt{x}<\texttt{K}
and
\texttt{y}<\texttt{K}

then

3:

return

\texttt{xorAr{}}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]

4:

else

5:

\ell\leftarrow\textsc{length}(\mathtt{max}(\texttt{x},\texttt{y}))/2

6:

\texttt{p}\leftarrow 2^{\ell}

7:

return

\texttt{p}\times\textsc{xor}(\texttt{x}/\texttt{p},\texttt{y}/\texttt{p})

+\;\textsc{xor}(\texttt{x}\;\mathtt{mod}\;\texttt{p},\texttt{y}\;\mathtt{mod}\;\texttt{p})

Time complexity.

We have the linearity of the preprocessing because
K=\left\lceil\sqrt{N} \right\rceil
and
thus the two nested loops, lines 7-10, take
O(N)
time.
The result of the preprocessing is an array such that

\texttt{xorAr{}}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]=\texttt{x}\;\;\mathtt{xor}\;\;\texttt{y}
for all
\texttt{x},\texttt{y}<K
.
When calling
\textsc{xor}(\texttt{x},\texttt{y})
,
either both arguments
\texttt{x},\texttt{y}
are smaller than
K
, or we recurse with two calls,

\textsc{xor}(\texttt{x}^{\prime},\texttt{y}^{\prime})
and
\textsc{xor}(\texttt{x}^{\prime\prime},\texttt{y}^{\prime\prime})
, where the length of the maximum of

\texttt{x}^{\prime}
and
\texttt{y}^{\prime}
(resp.
\texttt{x}^{\prime\prime}
and
\texttt{y}^{\prime\prime}
) is, at most, half the length of the maximum of
x
and
y
plus one.
Starting with
\texttt{x},\texttt{y}<N^{d}
, which implies
\texttt{x},\texttt{y}<K^{2d}
, we obtain,
for the number
L_{2}
defined as the smallest power of two greater than or equal to

\textsc{length}(\mathtt{max}(\texttt{x},\texttt{y}))
,
the inequalities
12
12

12

We use the general inequality

\textsc{length}(u\times v)\leq\textsc{length}(u)+\textsc{length}(v)
, for all integers
u,v
.

L_{2}<2\times\textsc{length}(\mathtt{max}(\texttt{x},\texttt{y}))\leq 2\times\textsc{length}(K^{2d})\leq 4d\times\textsc{length}(K)
, from which we deduce

L_{2}/(2^{\left\lceil\log_{2}d\right\rceil+2})\leq L_{2}/(4d)<\textsc{length}(K)
.

Therefore, the recursion depth is bounded by the number
\delta\coloneqq\left\lceil\log_{2}d\right\rceil+2
since, after repeatedly dividing
L_{2}
by two,
\delta
times,
L_{2}
becomes smaller than
\textsc{length}(K)
so that the operands of the
xor
procedure themselves become less than
K
.
This means that the
xor
procedure executes in constant time, for any fixed integer
d\geq 1
.

Getting other bitwise logical operators.

All the bitwise operations can be obtained in the same way.
To get the operation
\mathtt{and}
(resp.
\mathtt{or}
),
we only have to replace the
xorAr
array with an
andAr
(resp.
orAr
) array,
and the assignment
\texttt{xorAr{}}[\texttt{i}][\texttt{j}]\leftarrow(\texttt{i}+\texttt{j})\;\mathtt{mod}\;2
, which defines the Boolean
\mathtt{xor}
operation (line 6 of the preprocessing procedure), is replaced by the assignment
\texttt{andAr{}}[\texttt{i}][\texttt{j}]\leftarrow\texttt{i}\times\texttt{j}

(resp.
\texttt{orAr}[\texttt{i}][\texttt{j}]\leftarrow\mathtt{max}(\texttt{i},\texttt{j})
), defining the Boolean
\mathtt{and}

(resp.
\mathtt{or}
) operation.

8.2
Constant time and complexity of Turing machines and cellular automata

The relationships between the complexity of RAMs and that of other
major computation models, Turing machines and cellular automata, are
wide open issues. It is easy to verify that addition, subtraction and
all the string or logical operations studied in the previous
subsections are computable in linear time on multi-tape Turing
machines. On the other hand, it is known that multiplication is
computable in linear time on cellular
automata
[
36
,
33
,
64
]
.

A natural question arises: is it true that each operation computable
in linear time on a Turing machine or on a cellular automaton is
computable in constant time on a RAM with addition, with linear-time
preprocessing? In this subsection, we answer this question positively.

Since it is known and easy to verify that any problem computable in linear time on a multi-tape Turing machine is computable in linear time on a cellular automaton
[
80
,
84
]
, it suffices to prove the expected result for cellular automata.

First, we need some definitions and lemmas about one-dimensional cellular automata
[
51
,
84
]
.

Definition 6

(cellular automaton, configuration, computation)
.

•A
cellular automaton
(CA or
automaton
)

\mathcal{A}\coloneqq(Q,\delta)
consists of a finite set of
states

Q
and a
transition function

\delta:Q^{3}\to Q
.

•A
configuration

C
of the CA is a bi-infinite word
\cdots c_{-2}c_{-1}c_{0}c_{1}c_{2}\cdots\in Q^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we say that the cell
i
of the configuration
C
is in the state
c_{i}
.

•The
successor configuration
of
C
for the CA is

\delta(C)\coloneqq\cdots c^{\prime}_{-2}c^{\prime}_{-1}c^{\prime}_{0}c^{\prime}_{1}c^{\prime}_{2}\cdots
where
c^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\delta(c_{i-1},c_{i},c_{i+1})
, for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

•The
computation
of the CA
from any initial configuration
C
is the sequence of configurations
(\delta^{j}(C))_{j\geq 0}
defined by

\delta^{0}(C)\coloneqq C
and
\delta^{j+1}(C)\coloneqq\delta(\delta^{j}(C))
.

We are interested in finite word problems.

Definition 7

(word configuration)
.

The configuration of a cellular automaton

\mathcal{A}=(Q,\delta)
that represents a finite non-empty word
w\in\Sigma^{+}
over a finite alphabet

\Sigma\subset Q
is the bi-infinite word
\cdots\sharp\sharp w\sharp\sharp\cdots
,
simply denoted
\sharp w\sharp
and called a
word configuration
, where
\sharp\in Q\setminus\Sigma
is a special state (comparable to the “blank” symbol of a Turing machine).

Let us describe how “special” the state
\sharp
can be:

Definition 8

(permanent state on the right)
.

A state
\sharp
is
permanent on the right
for
a cellular automaton
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\delta)
if we have:
(1)
\delta(q,\sharp,\sharp)=\sharp
, for each
q\in Q
;
(2)
\delta(q_{-1},q,q_{1})\neq\sharp
, for each
q\in Q\setminus\{\sharp\}
and all
q_{-1},q_{1}\in Q
.

Remark 16
.

If
\sharp
is a permanent state on the right and

C
is a configuration having state
\sharp
on all cells of position
j\geq i

(i.e.
\forall j\geq i,c_{j}=\sharp
), then these cells remain in state
\sharp
in
\delta(C)
.
Also, note that the condition (2) of Definition
8
prevents the computation from creating new
\sharp
symbols.

Definition 9

(active cell, active interval)
.

A cell
i
is said to be
active
in a computation of a CA if its state
c_{i}
is not
\sharp
in at least one configuration
C
of the computation.
By Definition
8
, the set of active cells of the computation from a word configuration is an interval, called the
active interval
