Appendix: Proof of Theorem
3
of Subsection
8.2

Let us first recall the result.

Theorem

3
.

Let
d\geq 1
be a constant integer and let
\mathtt{op}:\mathbb{N}^{r}\to\mathbb{N}
be an operation of arity

r\geq 1
, computable in linear time on a cellular automaton (CA).
There is a RAM with addition, such that, for any input integer
N>0
:

1.

Pre-computation:
the RAM computes some tables in time
O(N)
;

2.

Operation:
from these pre-computed tables and after reading
r
integers
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}<N^{d}
, the RAM computes in constant time the integer
\mathtt{op}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\delta)
be a CA that computes in linear time an operation
\mathtt{op}
, i.e. the function

f_{\mathtt{op}}:X_{1};\cdots;X_{r}\mapsto\mathtt{op}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
.
Before building a RAM that simulates the CA, let us slightly tweak the CA input configuration.
In particular, we add the integer
N
to the input as a reference to the upper bound
N^{d}
of the operands
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}<N^{d}
.

Let us represent the
(r+1)
-tuple of integers
(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
, for
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}<N^{d}
, by the word, called

\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})\coloneqq u_{L-1}\cdots u_{1}u_{0}\in\Gamma^{L}
, on the alphabet

\Gamma\coloneqq\{0,1,\ldots,\gamma-1\}
where
\gamma\coloneqq 2^{r+1}
, which is defined as follows:

Length of the code:

L\coloneqq\mathtt{length}(N^{d})
;

Letters of the code:

u_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{r}x_{i,j}2^{i}
, for
0\leq j<L
,
where
x_{i,L-1}\dots x_{i,1}x_{i,0}
denotes the binary representation of
X_{i}
in
L
bits,
for
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,r\}
and
X_{0}\coloneqq N
. In other words,
u_{j}
is the integer whose binary representation is column
j
of the 0/1 matrix
(x_{i,j})_{0\leq i\leq r,0\leq j<L}
when the columns are numbered from
L-1
(left) downto 0 (right).

Example:
For
r=2
,
d=1
, and the size integer
N=13
, which has the binary representation
1101
of length
L=4
, and for
X_{1}=7
and
X_{2}=9
, which have the binary representations (of length
L=4
)
0111
and
1001
, respectively, we obtain
\gamma=2^{r+1}=8
,

\Gamma=\{0,1,\ldots,\gamma-1=7\}
and

\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})=5327\in\Gamma^{4}
, see Table
12
.

X_{2}=9

1

0

0

1

X_{1}=7

0

1

1

1

X_{0}=N=13

1

1

0

1

\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},X_{2})

u_{3}=5

u_{2}=3

u_{1}=2

u_{0}=7

Table 12:
Encoding the input
(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})

It is tedious but routine to build a cellular automaton that computes in linear time the function
g:\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})\mapsto X_{1};\cdots;X_{r}
.
By Lemma
15
, the composition of functions
f_{\mathtt{op}}\circ g:\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})\mapsto\mathtt{op}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
is computed in linear time on a CA, also called for simplicity
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\delta)
, with the set of output states
Q_{out}\subset Q
.
By Definition
10
supplemented by Lemma
17
and Remark
18
, this means that there exist a constant integer
c\geq 1
and a projection

\pi:Q_{out}\cup\{\sharp\}\to\{0,1,\sharp\}
such that,
for all integers
N>0
and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}<N^{d}
,
we have
\delta^{cL}(\sharp\mathtt{Input}\sharp)=\sharp Y\sharp
,

•

with
\mathtt{Input}\coloneqq\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})

and
L\coloneqq\mathtt{length}(\mathtt{Input})=\mathtt{length}(N^{d})
,

•

and for a word
Y=y_{cL-1}\cdots y_{0}\in(Q_{out})^{cL}
such that

\mathtt{Output}\coloneqq\pi(y_{cL-1})\cdots\pi(y_{0})\in\{0,1\}^{cL}

is the binary notation of the integer
\mathtt{op}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
written to
cL
bits.

We can assume
Q\coloneqq\{0,1,\ldots s-1\}
with
\Gamma\coloneqq\{0,1,\ldots,\gamma-1\}
,

\gamma\coloneqq 2^{r+1}
,
\sharp\coloneqq\gamma
,
and
\Gamma\cup\{\sharp\}\subseteq\penalty\ Q
, which implies
s\geq\gamma+1
.
For convenience, the active cells of the computation are numbered
cL-1,\ldots,1,0
, increasing from right to left (like the representation of integers in any base).
Thus, at any time
t\in[0,cL]
of the computation, the configuration has the form

\sharp q_{cL-1}\cdots q_{1}q_{0}\sharp
,
where
q_{i}\in Q
is the state of cell
i
(possibly
\sharp
) at time
t
.

We are now ready to present the simulation of the CA by a RAM.
Before describing it with all the necessary details, let us first give the guiding ideas of this simulation.

A simplified view of the simulation.

The trick is to divide the active interval of the computation, of length
cL
, into a constant number of consecutive blocks of “small” length
\ell
, called
\ell
-blocks, numbered
\ldots 2,1,0
(increasing from right to left!).
We take
\ell\coloneqq\epsilon\log_{2}N
for a “small” constant
\epsilon
. (Precisely, we will define
\ell\coloneqq\left\lceil L/D\right\rceil
, for a large fixed integer
D
.)
Similarly, we divide the computation, of time
cL
, into a constant number of computation intervals of duration
\ell
, called “global
\ell
-transitions”.

By abuse of language, the string of states
B_{j}^{t}\in Q^{\ell}
of an
\ell
-block at some time
t
is called an
\ell
-block at time
t
.
Therefore, the configuration at any time
t
is the concatenation
\sharp B_{c_{0}}^{t}\cdots B_{1}^{t}\sharp
, for the constant
c_{0}\coloneqq\left\lceil cL/\ell\right\rceil
.

The essential point is the “locality” of the cellular automaton: the state of any cell
x
at any time
t+1
(resp.
t+\ell
) is entirely determined by the states of the three cells
x-1,x,x+1
(resp. the state string of the cell interval
[x-\ell,x+\ell]
) at time
t
.
As a consequence, an
\ell
-block
B_{j}^{t+\ell}
, i.e. the
\ell
-block
j
at time
t+\ell
, is determined by the three

\ell
-blocks,
B_{j+1}^{t}
,
B_{j}^{t}
,
B_{j-1}^{t}
, which are the
\ell
-block itself and its left and right neighboring
\ell
-blocks at time
t
.
In other words, the
\ell
-block
B_{j}^{t+\ell}\in Q^{\ell}

is determined by the concatenation
B_{j+1}^{t}B_{j}^{t}B_{j-1}^{t}\in Q^{3\ell}
.
Such a concatenation is called a “local configuration” and the transformation

B_{j+1}^{t}B_{j}^{t}B_{j-1}^{t}\mapsto B_{j}^{t+\ell}
from
Q^{3\ell}
to
Q^{\ell}
is called a “local
\ell
-transition”.
There are two crucial points:

What preprocessing in linear time?

The number of possible “local configurations”, therefore the number of possible “local
\ell
-transitions” for the parameter
N
, called
\mathtt{NbLocalConf}(N)
, is the cardinality of the set
Q^{3\ell}
, which is “small”, precisely
s^{3\ell}
;
this will make it possible to pre-compute in
O(N)
time a table of all possible “local
\ell
-transitions” for the integer
N
;

How to compute
\mathtt{op}
in constant time?

Since the time (resp. length of the active interval) of the computation is
cL
, then the computation (resp. the active interval) is the concatenation of
cL/\ell=O(1)
global
\ell
-transitions (resp.
\ell
-blocks), and all the computation on
\mathtt{Input}\coloneqq\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
can be done by performing
(cL/\ell)^{2}=O(1)
local
\ell
-transitions; it is a constant time since each local
\ell
-transition is performed by consulting a single element in the table of local
\ell
-transitions.

Remark 22

(processing the input and output in constant time)
.

In this simplified view, we have not mentioned the reading of the operands
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}<N^{d}
, with the encoding process giving the initial configuration

\sharp\mathtt{Input}\sharp
, for
\mathtt{Input}\coloneqq\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
, nor the final output process building
\mathtt{op}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
by the projection of the final configuration.
We will explain below how these input and output processes can also be performed in constant time.

Remark 23
.

Our simplified view also overlooked the fact that in general, the time
cL
of the computation is not a multiple of
\ell
.
In general, we have
cL=(c_{0}-1)\ell+\rho
, for the “constant”
c_{0}\coloneqq\left\lceil cL/\ell\right\rceil
and an integer
\rho\in[1,\ell]
.
Therefore, the computation breaks down into
c_{0}-1
global
\ell
-transitions, each composed of
c_{0}
local
\ell
-transitions,
and one global
\rho
-transition composed of
c_{0}
local
\rho
-transitions of time
\rho
. Like a local
\ell
-transition, a local
\rho
-transition produces, from a local configuration
(B_{2},B_{1},B_{0})\in(Q^{\ell})^{3}
of adjacent
\ell
-blocks at some time
t
, the value
B^{\prime}_{1}\in Q^{\ell}
of the intermediate
\ell
-block at time
t+\rho
(instead of time
t+\ell
for a local
\ell
-transition).

The number of local configurations.

Let us now determine the value of the “cutting” constant
D
which determines the block length
\ell(N)\coloneqq\left\lceil L(N)/D\right\rceil
,
for
L(N)\coloneqq\mathtt{length}(N^{d})
,
so that the number
\mathtt{NbLocalConf}(N)

of the possible local configurations, for an input
\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
,
is
O(N^{\sigma})
, for some
\sigma<1
.
By definition of local configurations, we have

\mathtt{NbLocalConf}(N)=s^{3\ell}=s^{3\left\lceil L/D\right\rceil}<s^{3}s^{3L/D}

because of
\left\lceil L/D\right\rceil<L/D+1
.
We also have
L=\mathtt{length}(N^{d})\leq 1+\log_{2}N^{d}=1+d\log_{2}N
. It comes

s^{3L/D}\leq s^{3/D}s^{(3d/D)\log_{2}N}=s^{3/D}N^{(3d/D)\log_{2}s}
.
from which we deduce

\mathtt{NbLocalConf}(N)<s^{3+3/D}\times N^{(3d/D)\log_{2}s}

This implies
\mathtt{NbLocalConf}(N)<c_{1}N^{\sigma}
, for the constant factor
c_{1}\coloneqq s^{3+3/D}

and the constant exponent
\sigma\coloneqq(3d/D)\log_{2}s
,
which is less than 1 if we fix the value of our constant integer
D
to more than
3d\log_{2}s
.
Therefore, setting
D\coloneqq 1+\left\lfloor 3d\log_{2}s\right\rfloor
is suitable.

It is time to present in detail how a RAM can simulate in constant time the cellular automaton
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\delta)
thanks to pre-computed tables in linear time.
It is convenient to confuse the active part (resp. any
\ell
-block) of a computation of
\mathcal{A}
, which is a non-empty word over the alphabet
Q=\{0,1,\ldots,s-1\}
, with the integer represented by this word in the base
s
.
As we have already seen, we can easily go from a string of
s
-digits to the integer represented by this string in the base
s
, by Horner’s method, and vice versa.

Computation of local transition tables.

First, to represent the transition function
\delta
of the CA, let us define the 3-dimensional array

\mathtt{DELTA}[0..s-1][0..s-1][0..s-1]
by

\mathtt{DELTA}[x_{2}][x_{1}][x_{0}]\coloneqq\delta(x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})

The
\mathtt{DELTA}
array can be computed in constant time
s^{3}

since for each
(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in[0..s-1]^{3}
,
\delta(x_{2},x_{1},x_{0})
is an explicit integer.

Let us now represent the table of local
\rho
-transitions
B_{j+1}^{t}B_{j}^{t}B_{j-1}^{t}\mapsto B_{j}^{t+\ell}
from
Q^{3\ell}
to
Q^{\ell}
, for all the
\rho\in[1,\ell]
, by the 2-dimensional array

\texttt{LT}[1..\ell][0..s^{3\ell}-1]
defined, for

1\leq\rho\leq\ell
,
B_{0},B_{1},B_{2}\in Q^{\ell}
and
B=B_{2}s^{2\ell}+B_{1}s^{\ell}+B_{0}
, by

\texttt{LT}[\rho][B]\coloneqq R
,
which is interpreted as follows: if, at any time
t
, three consecutive
\ell
-blocks numbered
j+1
,
j
,
j-1
are respectively
B_{2},B_{1},B_{0}
, then at time
t+\rho
, the middle
\ell
-block (numbered
j
) is
R
.

The following code computes the
LT
array thanks to the
\mathtt{DELTA}
array:

Algorithm 21

Computation of the
LT
array

1:

for

\texttt{B}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{s}^{3\times\ell}-1

do

2:

for

\rho\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \ell

do

3:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow\texttt{B}

4:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ 3\times\ell-1

do

5:

\texttt{C}[\texttt{x}][0]\leftarrow\texttt{R}\;\mathtt{mod}\;\texttt{s}

6:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow\texttt{R}\;\mathtt{div}\;\texttt{s}

7:

for

\texttt{t}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \rho

do

8:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ \ell-\rho+\texttt{t}\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ 2\times\ell-1+\rho-\texttt{t}

do

9:

\texttt{C}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{t}]\leftarrow\texttt{DELTA}[\texttt{C}[\texttt{x}+1][\texttt{t}-1]][\texttt{C}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{t}-1]][\texttt{C}[\texttt{x}-1][\texttt{t}-1]]

10:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow 0

11:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 2\times\ell-1\penalty\ \textbf{downto}\penalty\ \ell

do

12:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow\texttt{R}\times\texttt{s}+\texttt{C}[\texttt{x}][\ell]

13:

\texttt{LT}[\rho][\texttt{B}]\leftarrow\texttt{R}

Justification:

First, note that the main loop (line 1) goes through the integers
B<s^{3\ell}
encoding local configurations (triplets of adjacent
\ell
-blocks).
Of course,
C[x,t]
represents the state of a cell
x\in[0,3\ell-1]
of the local configuration at an instant numbered
t\in[0,\rho]
of a time interval of duration
\rho
, and the main assignment (line 9) is a paraphrase of the transition function

\displaystyle C[x][t]=\delta(C[x+1][t-1],C[x][t-1],C[x-1][t-1])

(33)

The loop condition
x\in[\ell-\rho+t,\;2\ell-1+\rho-t]
of line 8 for the application of (
33
) is justified inductively by the locality of the transition function: obviously, the sequence of states at time
t
of the cell interval
[\ell-\rho+t,\;2\ell-1+\rho-t]

(which is equal to the middle
\ell
-block interval
[\ell,\;2\ell-1]
for
t=\rho
) are determined by the sequence of states at time
t-1
of the cell interval
[\ell-\rho+(t-1),\;2\ell-1+\rho-(t-1)]
, which has one more cell, numbered
\ell-\rho+t-1

(resp.
2\ell-1+\rho-t+1
), at each end.

In addition, lines 3-6 of the algorithm transform an integer
B<s^{3\ell}
(representing a local configuration) into the array of its
3\ell

s
-digits: the initial string
C[x][0]
, for
0\leq x\leq 3\ell-1
.

Conversely, lines 10-12 transform the final string
C[x][\rho]
, for
\ell\leq x\leq 2\ell-1
,
into the integer
R<s^{\ell}
it represents:
this is the resulting middle
\ell
-block of the local
\rho
-transition (line 13).

Representing the computation by a two-dimensional array of
\ell
-blocks.

We represent the computation of the CA by the sequence of its
c_{0}+1
global configurations

\mathcal{C}_{t\ell}\coloneqq\sharp B_{c_{0}}^{t\ell}\cdots B_{1}^{t\ell}\sharp
at time
t\ell
, for
0\leq t\leq c_{0}
,
where
B_{i}^{t\ell}
is the
i
th
\ell
-block,
1\leq i\leq c_{0}
, of
\mathcal{C}_{t\ell}
from the right.
Since we have
cL=(c_{0}-1)\ell+\rho
, for the constant
c_{0}\coloneqq\left\lceil cL/\ell\right\rceil
and an integer
\rho
such that

1\leq\rho\leq\ell
, note that, in case
\rho<\ell
, we need to add
\ell-\rho
symbols
\sharp
at the left of the
\rho
-block numbered
c_{0}
to make it an
\ell
-block.

For convenience, we add to each global configuration
\mathcal{C}_{t\ell}
two
\ell
-blocks,

B_{0}^{t\ell}\coloneqq\sharp^{\ell}
at its right, and
B_{c_{0}+1}^{t\ell}\coloneqq\sharp^{\ell}
at its left.
Thus, the global configuration
\mathcal{C}_{t\ell}
is now written

B_{c_{0}+1}^{t\ell}B_{c_{0}}^{t\ell}\cdots B_{1}^{t\ell}B_{0}^{t\ell}
.
Note that the string
\sharp^{\ell}
is assimilated to the integer represented in base
s
by
\ell
digits
\gamma
,

\overline{\gamma\cdots\gamma}
, which, by our convention
\sharp\coloneqq\gamma\coloneqq 2^{r+1}
,
is equal to
2^{r+1}(s^{\ell-1}+\cdots+s+1)=2^{r+1}(s^{\ell}-1)/(s-1)
.

Computing the initial configuration.

This is the most technical and most tedious part of the simulation.
Recall
cL=(c_{0}-1)\ell+\rho
, for the “constant”

c_{0}\coloneqq\left\lceil cL/\ell\right\rceil
and the integer
\rho
, which satisfies

1\leq\rho\leq\ell
. Similarly,
L=(c_{1}-1)\ell+\lambda
, for the “constant”
c_{1}\coloneqq\left\lceil L/\ell\right\rceil
and the integer
\lambda
, which satisfy
1\leq\lambda\leq\ell
and
c_{1}\leq c_{0}
.
(We write that
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
are “constant” in quotation marks because, even if they depend on
N
, they are bounded by constants: precisely, we have
c_{1}=\left\lceil L/\ell\right\rceil=\left\lceil L/\left\lceil L/D \right\rceil\right\rceil\leq\left\lceil L/(L/D)\right\rceil=D
and, likewise,
c_{0}\leq cD
.)
Also, recall the definition
\mathtt{code}(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})\coloneqq u_{L-1}\cdots u_{1}u_{0}\in\Gamma^{L}

where
u_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{r}x_{i,j}2^{i}
, for
0\leq j<L
, and
x_{i,L-1}\cdots x_{i,1}x_{i,0}
denotes the binary representation of
X_{i}
in
L
bits. In other words, the
j
th
\gamma
-digit
u_{j}
of

\mathtt{code}(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
,
where
\gamma\coloneqq 2^{r+1}
, is

\sum_{i=0}^{r}(j\mathtt{th}\;\mathtt{bit}\;\mathtt{of}\;X_{i})\times 2^{i}
.

We obtain the initial configuration
\mathcal{C}_{0}=B_{c_{0}+1}^{0}B_{c_{0}}^{0}\cdots B_{1}^{0}B_{0}^{0}
by the four following steps.

1.

Decompose each of the binary strings
X_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{L}
,
0\leq j\leq r
, as a concatenation
X_{i}=X_{c_{1},i}X_{c_{1}-1,i}\cdots X_{1,i}
of, from right to left,
c_{1}-1

\ell
-blocks
X_{1,i},\ldots,X_{c_{1}-1,i}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
a
\lambda
-block
X_{c_{1},i}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
;

2.

Compute the decomposition of
\mathtt{Input}\coloneqq\mathtt{code}(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})\in\Gamma^{L}
as a concatenation

U=U_{c_{1}}U_{c_{1}-1}\cdots U_{1}
of
c_{1}-1

\ell
-blocks
U_{1},\ldots,U_{c_{1}-1}\in\Gamma^{\ell}
and a
\lambda
-block
U_{c_{1}}\in\Gamma^{\lambda}
; here again, for each
h\in[1,c_{1}]
, we write
U_{h}=\mathtt{code}(X_{h,0},X_{h,1},\ldots,X_{h,r})
to mean that for each rank
i
,
0\leq i<\ell
,
the
i
th
\gamma
-digit of
U_{h}
is

\sum_{j=0}^{r}(i\mathtt{th}\;\mathtt{bit}\;\mathtt{of}\;X_{h,j})\times 2^{j}
;

3.

Compute the
\ell
-blocks
B_{j}^{0}
, for
j\in[1,c_{1}-1]
, of the global configuration

\mathcal{C}_{0}=B_{c_{0}+1}^{0}B_{c_{0}}^{0}\cdots B_{0}^{0}
at time 0;
note that, for each
j\in[1,c_{1}-1]
, the representation of
U_{j}
in base
\gamma
,
denoted
\overline{a_{\ell-1}\cdots a_{1}a_{0}}^{\gamma}
, “is” the representation of
B_{j}^{0}
in base
s
, i.e. the same string of digits:
B_{j}^{0}=\overline{a_{\ell-1}\cdots a_{1}a_{0}}^{s}
;
in terms of integers, this means that if we have
U_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}a_{i}\gamma^{i}
(
0\leq a_{i}\leq\gamma-1
),
then we obtain
B_{j}^{0}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}a_{i}s^{i}
, which we write

B_{j}^{0}=\mathtt{convert}_{\gamma\to s}(U_{j})
;

4.

Compute the other
\ell
-blocks of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
:

(a)

as seen above, we have

B_{j}^{0}=\sharp^{\ell}=2^{r+1}(s^{\ell}-1)/(s-1)
, for
j\in\{0\}\cup[c_{1}+1,c_{0}+1]

(recall
\sharp\coloneqq\gamma\coloneqq 2^{r+1}
);

(b)

finally, the representation of the integer
B_{c_{1}}^{0}
in the base
s
corresponds to the concatenation of strings
\sharp^{\ell-\lambda}
and
U_{c_{1}}
.
Thus we obtain

B_{c_{1}}^{0}=2^{r+1}(s^{\ell}-s^{\lambda})/(s-1)+U^{\prime}_{c_{1}}

with
U^{\prime}_{c_{1}}\coloneqq\mathtt{convert}_{\gamma\to s}(U_{c_{1}})
, which means that

U_{c_{1}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}a_{i}\gamma^{i}
(
0\leq a_{i}\leq\gamma-1
) implies

U^{\prime}_{c_{1}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}a_{i}s^{i}
.

To compute the initial configuration in constant time by the previous algorithm (1-4), we need to pre-compute two arrays in linear time.

Computing the tables
\mathtt{CODE}
and
\mathtt{CONVERT}
.

Let
\mathtt{CODE}[1..\ell][0..2^{\ell}-1][0..2^{\ell}-1]\cdots[0..2^{\ell}-1]
be the array defined, for each
\lambda\in[1,\ell]
and all
X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\in[0,2^{\lambda}-1]
, by

\mathtt{CODE}[\lambda][X_{0}],[X_{1}]\cdots[X_{r}]\coloneqq\mathtt{code}(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})

where
\mathtt{code}(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
is the integer in
[0,\gamma^{\lambda}-1]
(
\gamma=2^{r+1}
) defined by item 2 above.
The
\mathtt{CODE}
array is computed by the following algorithm:

Algorithm 22

Computation of the
CODE
array

1:

for

\lambda\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \ell

do

2:

for

(\texttt{X}_{0},\texttt{X}_{1},\ldots,\texttt{X}_{\texttt{r}})\in[0,2^{\lambda}-1]^{\texttt{r}+1}

do

3:

for

\texttt{j}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{r}

do

4:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \lambda-1

do

5:

\texttt{BIT}_{\texttt{j}}[\texttt{i}]\leftarrow\texttt{X}_{\texttt{j}}\;\mathtt{mod}\;2

6:

\texttt{X}_{\texttt{j}}\leftarrow\texttt{X}_{\texttt{j}}\;\mathtt{div}\;2

7:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \lambda-1

do

8:

\texttt{DIGIT}[\texttt{i}]\leftarrow 0

9:

for

\texttt{j}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ \texttt{r}\penalty\ \textbf{downto}\penalty\ 0

do

10:

\texttt{DIGIT}[\texttt{i}]\leftarrow 2\times\texttt{DIGIT}[\texttt{i}]+\texttt{BIT}_{\texttt{j}}[\texttt{i}]

11:

\texttt{V}\leftarrow 0

12:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ \lambda-1\penalty\ \textbf{downto}\penalty\ 0

do

13:

\texttt{V}\leftarrow\gamma\times\texttt{V}+\texttt{DIGIT}[\texttt{i}]

14:

\texttt{CODE}[\lambda][\texttt{X}_{0}][\texttt{X}_{1}]\cdots[\texttt{X}_{\texttt{r}}]\leftarrow\texttt{V}

Comment:

Lines 3-6 of the algorithm compute the
\lambda
bits of each integer

X_{0},\dots,X_{r}<2^{\lambda}
.
Lines 7-10 and Lines 11-14 compute respectively the
\lambda

\gamma
-digits
of the integer
\texttt{CODE}[\lambda][\texttt{X}_{0}][\texttt{X}_{1}]\cdots[\texttt{X}_{\texttt{r}}]

and its value.

Similarly, let
\mathtt{CONVERT}[1..\ell][0..\gamma^{\ell}-1]
be the array defined, for each length
\lambda\in[1,\ell]
and each integer
B\in[0,\gamma^{\lambda}-1]
, by

\mathtt{CONVERT}[\lambda][B]\coloneqq\mathtt{convert}_{\gamma\to s}(B)

where
\mathtt{convert}_{\gamma\to s}(B)
is the integer in
[0,s^{\lambda}-1]
defined by the following implication (see items 3 and 4 above):

B=\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}a_{i}\gamma^{i}
, for
0\leq a_{i}<\gamma
, implies

\mathtt{convert}_{\gamma\to s}(B)\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}a_{i}s^{i}
.
We can easily verify that the
\mathtt{CONVERT}
array is computed by the following algorithm:

Algorithm 23

Computation of the
CONVERT
array

1:

for

\lambda\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \ell

do

2:

for

\texttt{B}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \gamma^{\lambda}-1

do

3:

\texttt{U}\leftarrow\texttt{B}

4:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \lambda-1

do

5:

\texttt{DIGIT}[\texttt{i}]\leftarrow\texttt{U}\;\mathtt{mod}\;\gamma

6:

\texttt{U}\leftarrow\texttt{U}\;\mathtt{div}\;\gamma

7:

\texttt{U}\leftarrow 0

8:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ \lambda-1\penalty\ \textbf{downto}\penalty\ 0

do

9:

\texttt{U}\leftarrow\texttt{s}\times\texttt{U}+\texttt{DIGIT}[\texttt{i}]

10:

\texttt{CONVERT}[\lambda][\texttt{B}]\leftarrow\texttt{U}

Computing the output by projecting the final configuration.

This step is similar but much simpler than the computation of the initial configuration from the input.

For simplicity, we define
\pi(x)
for each
x\in Q
and not only for

x\in Q_{out}\cup\{\sharp\}
as it is necessary.
It also convenient to set
\pi(\sharp)\coloneqq 0
(instead of
\pi(\sharp)\coloneqq\sharp
) to take account of the possible
\sharp
symbols at the left end of the leftmost
\ell
-block, the
c_{0}
-th
\ell
-block.
More generally, we set
\pi:Q\to\{0,1\}
with
\pi(q)\coloneqq 0
for each
q\in Q\setminus Q_{out}
.

Here again, by its locality, the projection is done for each symbol
x\in Q
of each
\ell
-block of the final configuration. It uses the array
\mathtt{PROJECT}[0..s^{\ell}-1]
defined by

\mathtt{PROJECT}[B]\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\pi(x_{i})\times 2^{i}

where
B
is any
\ell
-block
x_{\ell-1}\cdots x_{1}x_{0}\in Q^{\ell}
, or equivalently,
B=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}x_{i}\times s^{i}
, for
x_{i}\in[0,s-1]
.
The
\mathtt{PROJECT}
array is computed by the following algorithm where
\mathtt{PI}[0..s-1]
denotes the array defined by

\mathtt{PI}[x]\coloneqq\pi(x)
, for
x\in Q=[0..s-1]
.

Algorithm 24

Computation of the
PROJECT
array

1:

for

\texttt{B}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{s}^{\ell}-1

do

2:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow\texttt{B}

3:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \ell-1

do

4:

\texttt{DIGIT}[\texttt{i}]\leftarrow\texttt{PI}[\texttt{R}\;\mathtt{mod}\;\texttt{s}]

5:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow\texttt{R}\;\mathtt{div}\;\texttt{s}

6:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow 0

7:

for

\texttt{i}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ \ell-1\penalty\ \textbf{downto}\penalty\ 0

do

8:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow 2\times\texttt{R}+\texttt{DIGIT}[\texttt{i}]

9:

\texttt{PROJECT}[\texttt{B}]\leftarrow\texttt{R}

Putting things together: simulation of the cellular automaton.

Recall that, for
cL=(c_{0}-1)\ell+\rho
with
1\leq\rho\leq\ell
, i.e. for

c_{0}\coloneqq\left\lceil cL/\ell\right\rceil
and
\rho\coloneqq cL-(c_{0}-1)\ell
,
the computation of the automaton
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\delta)
decomposes into

•

c_{0}-1
global
\ell
-transitions, each composed of
c_{0}
local
\ell
-transitions, and

•

one global
\rho
-transition composed of
c_{0}
local
\rho
-transitions of time
\rho
.

Let
B_{x}^{T}
be the
\ell
-block numbered
x
,
0\leq x\leq c_{0}+1
,
of the global configuration
\mathcal{C}_{T}
at time
T
,
0\leq T\leq cL
.
The
\mathtt{op}
operation is computed by the following procedure, called
op
, which simulates the automaton
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\delta)
with its projection
\pi:Q\to\{0,1\}
.
The main data structure of the
op
procedure is a two-dimensional array, called

\mathtt{BK}[0..c_{0}+1][0..c_{0}]
and defined by

\mathtt{BK}[x][t]\coloneqq B_{x}^{t\ell}
for
0\leq t\leq c_{0}-1
, and

\mathtt{BK}[x][c_{0}]\coloneqq B_{x}^{cL}
.

Algorithm 25

Constant-time computation of the
\mathtt{op}
operation

1:

procedure

op
(
\texttt{X}_{1},...,\texttt{X}_{\texttt{r}}
)

2:

\texttt{X}_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{N}

3:

for

\texttt{j}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{r}

do

4:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{c}_{1}

do

5:

\texttt{X}[\texttt{j}][\texttt{x}]\leftarrow\texttt{X}_{\texttt{j}}\;\mathtt{mod}\;2^{\ell}

6:

\texttt{X}_{\texttt{j}}\leftarrow\texttt{X}_{\texttt{j}}\;\mathtt{div}\;2^{\ell}

7:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{c}_{1}-1

do

\texttt{U}[\texttt{x}]\leftarrow\texttt{CODE}[\ell][\texttt{X}[0][\texttt{x}]]\cdots[\texttt{X}[\texttt{r}][\texttt{x}]]

9:

\texttt{U}[\texttt{c}_{1}]\leftarrow\texttt{CODE}[\lambda][\texttt{X}[0][\texttt{c}_{1}]]\cdots[\texttt{X}[\texttt{r}][\texttt{c}_{1}]]

10:

\texttt{BK}[0][0]\leftarrow 2^{\texttt{r}+1}\times(\texttt{s}^{\ell}-1)/(\texttt{s}-1)

for

\texttt{BK}[\texttt{c}_{1}][0]\leftarrow\texttt{CONVERT}[\lambda][\texttt{U}[\texttt{c}_{1}]]+2^{\texttt{r}+1}\times(\texttt{s}^{\ell}-\texttt{s}^{\lambda})/(\texttt{s}-1)

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ \texttt{c}_{1}+1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{c}_{0}+1

do

for

\texttt{t}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{c}_{0}-1

do

17:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{c}_{0}

do

18:

\texttt{BK}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{t}]\leftarrow\texttt{LT}[\ell][\textsc{Conc}(\texttt{BK}[\texttt{x}+1][\texttt{t}-1],\texttt{BK}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{t}-1],\texttt{BK}[\texttt{x}-1][\texttt{t}-1])]

19:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{c}_{0}

do

20:

\texttt{BK}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{c}_{0}]\leftarrow\texttt{LT}[\rho][\textsc{Conc}(\texttt{BK}[\texttt{x}+1][\texttt{c}_{0}-1],\texttt{BK}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{c}_{0}-1],\texttt{BK}[\texttt{x}-1][\texttt{c}_{0}-1])]

21:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow 0

22:

for

\texttt{x}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ \texttt{c}_{0}\penalty\ \textbf{downto}\penalty\ 1

do

23:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow\texttt{s}^{\ell}\times\texttt{R}+\texttt{PI}[\texttt{BK}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{c}_{0}]]

return

R

Comments:
Lines 2-15 (the major part of the
op
procedure!) construct the part of the

BK
array,
BK[x][0]
,
0\leq x\leq c_{0}+1
,
that represents the initial configuration
\mathcal{C}_{0}
of
\mathcal{A}

on the input
\mathtt{code}(N,X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
. More precisely, the construction decomposes into the following steps:

•

from the integers
X_{0}\coloneqq N,

X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}
, lines 2-6 of the
op
procedure compute the array
\texttt{X}[0..r][1..c_{1}]
such that
\texttt{X}[i][x]
is the
x
-th bit of
X_{i}
;

•

from the array
\texttt{X}[0..r][1..c_{1}]
, lines 7-9 compute the array
\texttt{U}[1..c_{1}]
such that
\texttt{U}[x]
is the
x
-th
\gamma
-digit of

\mathtt{code}(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{r})
(for
\gamma\coloneqq 2^{r+1}
), which is
\texttt{U}[x]=\sum_{i=0}^{r}2^{i}\times\texttt{X}[i][x]
;

•

from the
\texttt{U}[1..c_{1}]
array, lines 10-15 construct the part
\mathtt{BK}[0..c_{0}+1][0]
of the

\mathtt{BK}
array that encodes the initial configuration

\mathcal{C}_{0}=\sharp B^{0}_{c_{0}+1}\cdots B^{0}_{1}B^{0}_{0}\sharp
with the
\ell
-blocks

B^{0}_{x}
among which

–

B^{0}_{0}
and
B^{0}_{c_{1}+1},\ldots,B^{0}_{c_{0}+1}
are equal to the string
\sharp^{\ell}
encoded by the integer
2^{r+1}(s^{\ell}-1)/(s-1)
(line 10 and lines 14-15, respectively, corresponding to item 4.a above), and

–

corresponding to the “input”, each
\mathtt{BK}[j]
,
1\leq j\leq c_{1}-1
, is the integer whose string of
(
\ell
) digits in base
s
is equal to
B^{0}_{j}
(lines 11-12 corresponding to item 3),
and
\mathtt{BK}[c_{1}]
is the integer whose string of
(
\ell
) digits in base
s
is
B^{0}_{c_{1}}=\sharp^{\ell-\lambda}U_{c_{1}}
(line 13 corresponding to item 4.b; recall that
\lambda
is the length of
U_{c_{1}}
).

Lines 16-20 simulate the computation of
\mathcal{A}
: the
c_{0}-1
global
\ell
-transitions (lines 16-18) and the final global
\rho
-transition (lines 19-20).

Finally, lines 21-24 compute and return the output integer by projecting the final configuration
\mathcal{C}_{cL}
with the
\pi
projection.

It is immediate to check that the body of each “for” loop of the
op
procedure is repeated a number of times bounded by
r+1
,
c_{0}
or
c_{1}
, therefore by a constant, and therefore the
op
procedure executes in constant time as claimed.

Why is the preprocessing time linear?

Our constant-time procedure
op
uses five pre-computed tables:
\mathtt{PI}
, computed in constant time,
and
LT
,
CODE
,
CONVERT
and
PROJECT
.
By examining the structure of the loops of the above algorithms, it is easy to verify that

•

LT
is computed in time
O(\mathtt{NbLocalConf}(N)\times\ell^{3})=O(N^{\sigma}\times(\log N)^{3})=O(N)

(recall
\mathtt{NbLocalConf}(N)=s^{3\ell}=O(N^{\sigma})
, for a fixed
\sigma<1
),

•

CODE
and
CONVERT
are computed in time

O((2^{\ell})^{r+1}\times\ell^{2})=O(\gamma^{\ell}\times\ell^{2})
,
and
O(\gamma^{\ell}\times\ell^{2})
, respectively, and therefore in time
O(s^{\ell}\times\ell^{2})
, and

PROJECT
is computed in time
O(s^{\ell}\times\ell
), and therefore, a fortiori, all these tables are computed in time
O(N)
.

This completes the proof of Theorem
3
.
∎

12
Appendix: Another constant-time algorithm for division

While multiplication can be computed in linear time on a cellular automaton, so Theorem
3
applies to it, it is unknown if division is.
However, the intuitive principle of the usual division algorithm is also “local”: to compute the most significant (leftmost) digit of the quotient of integers
\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor
, the first step is to “try” to divide the integer
a
consisting of the 1, 2, 3, or 4 leftmost digits of
A
by the integer
b
consisting of the 1, 2, or 3 leftmost digits of
B
.

We are going to design a new constant-time algorithm for division by a careful adaptation of the usual division principle combined with a judicious choice of the base
K
in which the integers are represented.
To avoid any vicious circle here, we will only use the addition, subtraction, multiplication and base change of “polynomial” integers, and the procedure
\textsc{DivBySmall}(A,B)
of Subsection
5.1
, which returns
\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor
for
A<\beta^{d}
,
0<B<\beta
, for a fixed integer
d\geq 1
and
\beta\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/6}\right\rceil^{3}
.

Analyzing and optimizing the usual division algorithm.

Assume the dividend
A
and the divisor
B
are represented in any fixed base
K\geq 2
and let
a
(resp.
b
) be the integer represented by the 1, 2, 3, or 4 (resp. 1, 2, or 3) leftmost digits of
A
(resp.
B
). Each positive integer is assimilated to the string
\overline{x_{p-1}\cdots x_{1}x_{0}}
of digits
x_{i}
,
0\leq x_{i}<K
, which represents it in base
K
, with leading digit
x_{p-1}>0
. Clearly, we get the inequalities

aK^{m}\leq A<(a+1)K^{m}
and
bK^{n}\leq B<(b+1)K^{n}
, for some integers
a,b\geq 1
and
m,n
.
Because of
A\geq B
, we can assume
m\geq n
. It implies

\frac{a}{b+1}K^{m-n}<A/B<\frac{a+1}{b}K^{m-n}
.
To do
one
test instead of several tests to get the
most significant
digit of the quotient

A/B
in base
K
, we want the difference between the bounds to be less than
K^{m-n}
, it means

\frac{a+1}{b}-\frac{a}{b+1}\leq 1
.
Noticing the identity
\frac{a+1}{b}-\frac{a}{b+1}=\frac{a}{b(b+1)}+\frac{1}{b}
, this can be rephrased as

\displaystyle\frac{a}{b(b+1)}+\frac{1}{b}\leq 1.

(34)

Lemma 18
.

Assume
aK^{m}\leq A<(a+1)K^{m}
and
bK^{n}\leq B<(b+1)K^{n}
, for some integers
K\geq 2
, and
A,B
such that

A>B\geq 1
, and
m,n,a,b
such that
m\geq n\geq 0
, and
a>b\geq 1
, and
\frac{a}{b(b+1)}+\frac{1}{b}\leq 1
.
We then get

qK^{m-n}\leq A/B<(q+1)K^{m-n}
for
q=\left\lfloor\frac{a}{b+1}\right\rfloor
or
q=\left\lfloor\frac{a}{b+1}\right\rfloor+1
,

which implies
A=BqK^{m-n}+R
for some integer

R<BK^{m-n}
, and therefore

\displaystyle\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor=qK^{m-n}+\left\lfloor R/B\right\rfloor

(35)

with
q\geq 1
and
\left\lfloor R/B\right\rfloor<K^{m-n}
.

Proof.

From the inequalities
\frac{a}{b+1}K^{m-n}<A/B<\frac{a+1}{b}K^{m-n}
we deduce

\left\lfloor\frac{a}{b+1}\right\rfloor K^{m-n}<A/B<\left\lceil\frac{a+1}{b}\right\rceil K^{m-n}
.
Besides, the inequality
\frac{a+1}{b}-\frac{a}{b+1}\leq 1
implies
\left\lceil\frac{a+1}{b}\right\rceil\leq\left\lfloor\frac{a}{b+1}\right\rfloor+2
.
This yields
qK^{m-n}<A/B<(q+2)K^{m-n}
for
q=\left\lfloor\frac{a}{b+1}\right\rfloor\geq 1
, which proves the lemma.
∎

Remark 24
.

Lemma
18
gives and justifies the principle of a recursive division algorithm in any fixed base
K\geq 2
:
to compute
\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor
, compute the integers
q
and
\left\lfloor R/B\right\rfloor
in base
K
;
the representation of the integer
\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor
in base
K
is the concatenation of the string representing the integer
q\geq 1
and the string of
m-n
digits representing the integer
\left\lfloor R/B\right\rfloor<K^{m-n}
(possibly with leading zeros).

How to use Lemma
18
?

How many leftmost (most significant) digits of
A
and
B
, written in base
K
, should we take to obtain integers
a
and
b
, with
a\geq b\geq 1
, that satisfy the condition
\frac{a}{b(b+1)}+\frac{1}{b}\leq 1
of Lemma
18
? This is expressed precisely by the following question:
for which integers
i,j
is the implication

K^{i}\leq a<K^{i+1}\land K^{j}\leq b<K^{j+1}\Rightarrow\frac{a}{b(b+1)}+\frac{1}{b}\leq 1

true for all
a,b
?

Lemma 19
.

Let
i,j
be two positive integers. Then the condition
i<2j
is equivalent to the condition

\forall(a,b)\in[K^{i},K^{i+1}[\times[K^{j},K^{j+1}[\;\;\left(\frac{a}{b(b+1)}+\frac{1}{b}\leq 1\right)

Proof.

The maximum of the numbers

\frac{a}{b(b+1)}+\frac{1}{b}
for all pairs of integers
(a,b)\in[K^{i},K^{i+1}[\times[K^{j},K^{j+1}[

is
\frac{K^{i+1}-1}{K^{j}(K^{j}+1)}+\frac{1}{K^{j}}=\frac{K^{i+1}+K^{j}}{K^{2j}+K^{j}}
, which is at most 1 if and only if
i+1\leq 2j
.
This proves the lemma.
∎

We are now ready to give our new division algorithm using the principle given by Lemma
18
. Here again, we must choose carefully the base
K
.

The choice of the base.

We take
K\coloneqq\left\lceil N^{1/7}\right\rceil

and we assume that each “polynomial” operand
X<N^{d}
, for a fixed integer
d
, is written

\overline{x_{m-1}\cdots x_{1}x_{0}}
,
0\leq x_{i}\leq K-1
, in base
K
, with one digit
x_{i}
per register,
and is therefore stored in
m\leq 7d
registers, since
X<N^{d}\leq K^{7d}
. In particular, the following functions can be computed in constant time for their argument
X
so represented:

\mathtt{length}_{\mathtt{K}}(X)\coloneqq m
;

\mathtt{Substring}_{\mathtt{K}}(X,i,j)\coloneqq\overline{x_{i-1}\cdots x_{j}}
, for
m\geq i>j\geq 0
.

Notation
.

For an integer
X
written
\overline{x_{m-1}\cdots x_{1}x_{0}}
in base
K
,
0\leq x_{i}\leq K-1
, and a positive integer
p\leq m
, denote by
X_{(p)}
the prefix of the
p
most significant digits of
X
:

X_{(p)}\coloneqq\overline{x_{m-1}\cdots x_{m-p}}=X\;\mathtt{div}\;K^{m-p}
.

The division algorithm.

Let
A,B
be two “polynomial” integers such that
0<B\leq A<K^{d}
, for a fixed integer
d
. To divide
A
by
B
, we consider four cases:

1.

B<K^{2}
;

2.

K^{p}\leq B\leq A<K^{p+1}
, for an integer
p\geq 2
, and
A_{(3)} =B_{(3)}
;

3.

K^{2}\leq B\leq A
and
A_{(3)}>B_{(3)}
;

4.

K^{p}\leq B<K^{p+1}\leq A
, for an integer
p\geq 2
, and
A_{(3)} \leq B_{(3)}
.

Obviously, these four cases are mutually exclusive.
To ensure that there is no other case than these four cases, just note that if condition 2 is false when
K^{2}\leq B\leq A<K^{d}
, then either we have
A_{(3)}>B_{(3)}
(case 3), or we have

A_{(3)}\leq B_{(3)}

and

A
has
more
digits than
B
in base
K
(case 4).

•

Case 1 is handled by the procedure
\textsc{DivBySmall}(A,B)
of Subsection
5.1

(note that
K^{2}=\left\lceil N^{1/7}\right\rceil^{2}\leq\left\lceil N^{1/6}\right\rceil^{3}=\beta
).

•

In case 2, we clearly get
A-B<K^{p-2}<B
, which implies
A<2B
and therefore
\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor=1
.

•

Cases 3 and 4 are handled by Lemma
18
.
In case 3 (resp. case 4), take
a=A_{(3)}
and
b=B_{(3)}

(resp.
a=A_{(4)}
and
b=B_{(3)}
), which implies

K^{2}\leq b<a<K^{3}
(resp.
K^{2}\leq b<K^{3}\leq a<K^{4}
).

The integers
i=2
and
j=2
(resp.
i=3
and
j=2
) satisfy the condition
i<2j
of Lemma
19
. Thus, in cases 3 and 4, the condition
\frac{a}{b(b+1)}+\frac{1}{b}\leq 1
of Lemma
18
is satisfied.
Therefore, Lemma
18
proves the correctness of the following
Division
procedure which implements cases 1-4 for
A\geq B
. More precisely, the lines 17-21 of the algorithm compute

\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor
in cases 3 and 4 by using the pre-computed array
\texttt{DIV}[0..K^{4}-1][2..K^{3}]
defined by
\texttt{DIV}[x][y]\coloneqq\left\lfloor x/y\right\rfloor
, for
0\leq x<K^{4}
and
2\leq y\leq K^{3}

(see the pre-computation code of the
DIV
array presented after the
Division
procedure).

\mathtt{division}
operation

Division
(
\texttt{A},\texttt{B}
)

if

\texttt{A}<\texttt{B}

then

0

a\leftarrow A_{(4)}
, so
b=B_{(3)}<a<(b+1)K

16:

\texttt{m}\leftarrow\ell_{\texttt{A}}-4

17:

\texttt{q}\leftarrow\texttt{DIV}[\texttt{a}][\texttt{b}+1]

\triangleright

b+1\leq a<(b+1)K
and therefore
1\leq q<K

\triangleright
common treatment of cases 3 and 4 justified by Lemma
18

18:

if

\texttt{A}\geq\texttt{B}\times(\texttt{q}+1)\times\texttt{K}^{\texttt{m}-\texttt{n}}

then

19:

\texttt{q}\leftarrow\texttt{q}+1

20:

\texttt{R}\leftarrow\texttt{A}-\texttt{B}\times\texttt{q}\times\texttt{K}^{\texttt{m}-\texttt{n}}

21:

return

\texttt{q}\times\texttt{K}^{\texttt{m}-\texttt{n}}+\textsc{Division}(\texttt{R},\texttt{B})

\triangleright
justified by equality
35

Comments:

In case 4 (
a=A_{(3)}\leq B_{(3)}=b
), after the assignment
a\leftarrow A_{(4)}
of line 15, we have
a=A_{(4)}<(A_{(3)}+1)K
.
Now, by the hypothesis
A_{(3)}\leq B_{(3)}=b
(line 14), we get
b=B_{(3)}<a=A_{(4)}<(A_{(3)}+1)K\leq(b+1)K
, and thus
b<a<(b+1)K
, as the comment of line 15 claims.

In case 3, i.e. for
a=A_{(3)}>B_{(3)}=b
, we also have
a=A_{(3)}<B_{(4)}<(B_{(3)}+1)K=(b+1)K
.
This justifies, for cases 3 and 4, the assertion given as a comment of line 17:
b+1\leq a<(b+1)K
.

It remains to describe the preprocessing and to prove that the claimed complexity is achieved.

Pre-computation of the
\mathtt{DIV}
array.

Note that in the

Division
procedure, we compute
q=\left\lfloor a/(b+1)\right\rfloor
when
2\leq b+1\leq K^{3}
and
1\leq q\leq K-1
. Therefore, the following algorithm constructs the
\mathtt{DIV}
array.

Algorithm 27

Pre-computation of the
DIV
array

1:

for

\texttt{y}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 2\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{K}^{3}

do

2:

for

\texttt{q}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 1\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{K}-1

do

3:

for

\texttt{r}\penalty\ \textbf{from}\penalty\ 0\penalty\ \textbf{to}\penalty\ \texttt{y}-1

do

4:

\texttt{x}\leftarrow\texttt{q}\times\texttt{y}+\texttt{r}

5:

\texttt{DIV}[\texttt{x}][\texttt{y}]\leftarrow\texttt{q}

Complexity of the preprocessing and division algorithm.

We immediately see that the time of the above algorithm which constructs the array
\texttt{DIV}[0..K^{4}-1][2..K^{3}]

is
O(K^{3}\times K\times K^{3})=O(K^{7})=O(N)
.

The complexity of the
Division
procedure is determined by its recursive depth (number of recursive calls).
By equation
35
(implemented by line 21), which is
\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor=qK^{m-n}+\left\lfloor R/B\right\rfloor

with
q\geq 1
and
\left\lfloor R/B\right\rfloor<K^{m-n}
, the quotient
\left\lfloor R/B\right\rfloor
has at least one digit less than
\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor
when represented in base
K
(the digit
q
, multiplied by
K^{m-n}
). Therefore, the recursive depth is bounded by the number of digits of the representation of
\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor
in base
K
, which is less than
7d
because of
\left\lfloor A/B\right\rfloor<K^{7d}
.
Since the most time consuming instruction is the multiplication, executed
O(d)
times (lines 18, 20, 21) and whose time cost is
O(d^{2})
, the time of the
Division
procedure is
O(d^{3})
, at compare to the time
O(d^{4})
of the
Division
procedure of Section
5
, see Theorem
2
.
